#6889. The 6 Best Smart Cameras for Building Out Your Home Security

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

You can’t buy total peace of mind, but you can get pretty close.

The open doors era of American life is well and truly behind us. Instead, we have apps on our phones that notify us when a package gets delivered (or stolen) or when your kid gets home. You can have these things set up to be as jumpy as you are, detecting every little unknown motion in the driveway. Sorry, just a chipmunk.

In reality, nothing will give you total peace, but you can come pretty damn close with the help of a smart security camera. It doesn’t even take a full ADP system anymore. All you need is a one-time camera purchase, maybe a cheap subscription, and a phone—which you already have. At the very least, it's a whole lot better than leaving your front light on to deter intrusions while you're away for the week.

If you're in the market for a new security camera or video doorbell, you've come to the right place. Any of these options will keep you, your loved ones, and (most importantly) your treasured, and very expensive, belongings safe. From doorbell cams to floodlight cams to wild ones that you stick on your window, these are the six best (and smartest) home security cameras out there.

When I'm setting up an outdoor security camera, there are only three things I care about: Durability, battery life, and storage. Everything else—like smart recognition, video quality, and notifications—doesn’t matter as much. Give me 1080p resolution, an app that crashes all the time, and no advanced AI, and honestly, I really won’t mind. Thankfully, this camera is a bit better than that with an app that, for the record, does not crash all the time.

Arlo's Pro wireless camera is great in all three areas. First, it's properly weather resistant. It can handle storms, heat, and cold without any dips in performance. Second, without an Arlo Smart subscription ($3 a month for unlimited video storage) you can store one-week's worth of video on the cloud, which gives you more than enough time to download videos of particular events of interest. I wish there was some free local storage (even a couple gigs is enough to cycle recent footage), but at least the fee is reasonable.

It’s solid on the battery front too, with rechargeable batteries that only need to be swapped out about once every month. If you want to pay $199, you can get the version of the camera with an extra large battery, just note it is a bit bulkier.

Budget options don't get better than the Wyze cam. It plays nice with Google Assistant and Alexa, and the newest version boasts improved image quality and better night vision. This 2.5K resolution camera technically has a higher resolution than the Arlo Pro, which is nearly three times the price. What you’re paying for there is a wireless connection. This camera is entirely wired, needing to be plugged in to operate. As a result, the Wyze cam continues to be an excellent option for indoor security, but not one I’d recommend for outdoor usage.

You can pay Wyze for cloud storage, but it’s not the only option this camera gives. I love that Wyze allows users to bring their own MicroSD cards and use them for local storage. Chances are you have one somewhere, and these days ones with massive amounts of storage come relatively cheap. That truly makes this the budget smart camera option for indoor security.

If you want a nice-looking high-performance video doorbell, the new Nest is pretty much the full package. It always sends a crisp pitch right to your phone, and the camera itself has a wide enough field of view that you never feel like you’re missing something just out of frame. And if wired isn’t your speed, you can also grab a battery-powered version.

While I always appreciate a free cloud storage option, Google’s is pathetic. I can’t rightfully say it’s bad on purpose, but after a week of only being able to save 10 second clips you’ll be begging to give your $10 a month for Google Home Premium. This also unlocks advanced AI face detection features that make the base experience feel that much worse.

For apartments, townhomes, or anyone who already has a peephole, this is the perfect doorbell camera. Just pop out your current peephole and slide this one in. It’s really simple as that, no bullshit.

The only issue, as with any wireless camera, is battery life. Since this one is guaranteed to get a lot of traffic in front of it, battery changes are going to be pretty frequent, about every three weeks. That said, it's not a huge deal. The camera comes with two rechargeable battery packs, so you just have to make sure the spare is always charged and ready to go. At some point in the three week period, you’re bound to find the time.

The unfortunate downside of going with Ring is the subscription fee. There’s no free cloud storage so you’re going to be writing (another) annual check to Amazon if you want to use this one.

If you need a camera and floodlight combo, you have tons of options from every brand that makes these things. Any worthwhile one is going to be wired, that’s just how it is. Most of those aren’t bad, but Ring’s is especially good. It’s a wide-angle 2K video, with a 6X zoom, more than enough to get important details when you need them. The lights themselves are bright and sensitive (although not overly sensitive because that’s straight up annoying). The rest of it works as you’d expect, with all the controls and custom features the Ring app offers at your disposal.

The storage is both the best and worst thing about this camera. While I’ve complained about the lack of free storage on Ring devices already in this article, your $5 a month does go a long way. For some people that will be better than having to deal with local storage solutions. Still, I wish Ring offered the option.

So you want something you can move around whenever—maybe from the nursery to the backyard and back three times a week? For you, Eufy makes an adaptable window camera that mounts to your window using suction. Once you have it secured, you can use the knob to easily adjust the rotation. And, when it’s time to move it all you do is remove the Velcro mount and place it on the desired window on the other side of the house.

For something like a floodlight camera, I don’t find the wired setup to be a huge imposition for many people. With something like this window camera, being tethered via USB cord limits where you can place it. Depending on your home, this may or may not end up being an issue you need to account for.

What Do We Look for in Home Security Cameras?

Really, it boils down to a few things. Most importantly, in my mind, are durability, power, and video storage, but things like video quality, smart home compatibility, and motion detection also play a role.

Durability - How weather resistant is the camera? Here, all the cameras shown are IP65 or higher, meaning they're just about waterproof. I wouldn't go for any less.

Power - I'd always recommend going wired here. Wireless, especially for high-up security cameras, can be a bit of a pain to keep charged. Though, a lot of people prefer the ease of setup for wireless.

Video storage - Want unlimited cloud storage? It'll cost you. However, most cameras have some amount of complimentary storage. I wouldn't recommend anything under a week's worth of digital storage. And even then, I'd prefer a local option, as it's cheaper and easier to deal with.

Video quality - 1080p on a doorbell is fine. 2K on a security camera gets the job done. 2.5k or 3K video is where you want to be. 4k video is nice to have, but honestly it’s a bit overkill.

Motion detection - Most cameras are just OK at this. Brands claim their cameras are smart and can tell the difference between you and the UPS guy, but often that's a lie. At this price point, you just want something that can tell the difference between human and car. After that, it just needs to reliably detect motion and capture it.

#6885. House Republicans Couldn’t Have Picked a Worse Minority Speaker

So much for cleaning house. Rep. Jim Jordan of Ohio has been a loyal foot soldier of the MAGA regime for years now.

Politico has a very strange story about the battle for Republican leadership in the House of Representatives. Speaker Mike Johnson already has scratched himself from the race to become the next minority leader. And here is Politico’s take, which seems very, very odd.

So far, this is a run-of-the-mill state of the losers’ postelection story. The losing side, particularly a catastrophically losing side, always spends some time in public introspection. It’s almost a kind of penance. But here’s where the story spins out.

As I’m sure Stutzman knows, on any issue that both involves the administration and drove voters in the midterm elections, there’s not a dime’s worth of difference between Scalise and Jordan (R-Van Heusen). They have both been loyal foot soldiers of the MAGA regime. Both of them supported at least one objection to certify the results in 2020. On Tuesday, Jordan thumped Scalise and became the GOP leader, 106–73, and it will be interpreted as another defeat for Johnson’s old leadership team. But the promotion of Jordan doesn’t answer any of the problems that contributed to the loss of Congress by the Republicans. Jordan’s knees haven’t stopped jerking since 2017. He hunted every snipe at which the administration pointed, even the ones that didn’t exist, which was a substantial number of them. He was rude and approximately as committed to bipartisanship as a manhole cover. If the primary issue in the election was corruption, Jordan has made a career out of supplying alibis.

I hope Speaker-in-Waiting Jeffries realizes all of this. This is the legislative equivalent of Stephen Miller running the White House. I’d say the Republicans could have found someone more dedicated to getting things done, but there isn’t anybody like that left.

#6880. It’s Always Daiquiri Season

The simple, rum-based cocktail has been compromised by identity theft. So what is it, really?

The daiquiri has been compromised by identity theft. Down in Louisiana, the New Orleans Original Daiquiris chain has sullied the classic cocktail’s good name by slinging slushy sugar bombs with outrageous names like the Banana Banshee, Mudslide, and Crawgator.

Savvy Esquire readers know that the original drink involves nothing more than rum, lime juice, and sweetener. Yet modern specialists like Vay Su, cofounder of Vandell in Los Angeles, can take you on a Caribbean odyssey by ruminating on the minutiae: What kind of limes, and how fresh? Cane sugar or simple syrup? Shaved ice or a fat chunk? And how much of the rum character should force its way through?

Restaurants like Kabawa in New York’s East Village have transformed the humble daiquiri into an unforgettable drinking experience. When you step in and spy other people enjoying a concoction poured over 70 heaping grams of shaved ice, you already know what you’re going to order. Kathryn “Pepper” Stashek, Kabawa’s creative bartender—yes, that’s really her title—never gets tired of seeing that look in everyone’s eyes that says, Obviously, I need a daiquiri.

At Kabawa, you get to choose your rum too. Opt for the Jamaica–Barbados blend of Probitas or go agricole with Montebello Rhum Blanc from the islands of Guadeloupe. Maybe veer down a vintage alley with Bacardi Carta Blanca from 1978. You can’t go wrong; the only mistake is assuming you need to wait for warm weather. As Su says, it’s always daiquiri season.

Want to whip up a classic daiquiri at home? Here’s how to do it the Esquire way.

#6876. You Don’t Need a Smartwatch. You Need This $55 Timex.

The Ironman is a no-fuss watch that’ll help you get in shape without overcomplicating it.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Welcome to the Esquire Endorsement. Heavily researched. Thoroughly vetted. These picks are the best way to spend your hard-earned cash.

I’m not trying to run a marathon here. I just want to feel strong, feel healthy in my normal life. In fact, a flight of stairs was my tipping point. It was midsummer. I hit a jog up the stairs—for fun, just to move—coming out of my usual subway stop below the Esquire offices in Manhattan. Two long flights, maybe 50 steps total. I got to the top, step onto the street, and ... what the hell. I was gassed. Hands on my knees on a public sidewalk, gassed. I desperately needed to get back in the gym.

Let me clarify before friends and family text me to call bullshit, I had not let myself go; I was just a little out of shape. No one could tell, except for my older brother—he has been my tiger mom since I was born—who made sure to tell me after he invited my wife and me out for a Fire Island beach day. He could tell, and my lungs could tell—that’s what matters. Prior to that 30-second jog, I’d tried all the smart fitness trackers that are meant to game you into working out. None stuck. Life was too busy, my willpower was too weak. I wasn’t hitting the gym.

Then I bought a Timex Ironman. My thought, a stroke of genius in hindsight, was to have it within reach as much as possible. My workouts got idiot-proof, simple beyond belief: Manchester United’s 20-minute sprint test, kettlebell circuits, push-up and pull-up splits, or some form of lower-body lifting. Am I in the best shape of my life? No. But I could run a 5K right now, no problem. I’m back to basics and in good-enough shape. That’s all because of this watch.

If you are one of those high-achieving men that feels the need to run marathons, hike ultras, or cycle hundreds of miles, your qualm with a Timex Ironman won’t be the durability. “Ironman” does, in fact, come from the idea that this would be your companion over 140 miles of swim-bike-run. It’s water-resistant to 100 meters, plenty for swimming laps. It has a chronograph, alarm, and timer—the only things you actually need to track a workout. Want to know if your VO2 max is getting better? Or your lifts are paying off? Track numbers: Splits get faster, reps get higher. You don’t need all the heart-rate stuff.

And, crucially, there isn’t much to break down on the inside.

But suppose your Timex Ironman does break down. Unless you got an anomaly or wear it into literal war, this shouldn’t happen for about a decade; my dad has owned one Ironman since before I was born in the 90s. Even if you only hit five to seven years, that’s longer than a smartwatch lasts before getting phased out because of software improvements. That’s a $500 replacement. An Ironman is a tenth of that.

It’s also worth saying that I do not have an issue with smartwatches or fitness trackers; they are truly revolutionary when it comes to catching hidden health problems. And if it’s your jam to track steps, count calories, and see little rings that tell you about VO2 max and sleep scores—by all means, do what works for you. But that line of thinking just does not work on me. A screen on my wrist means I’m constantly fiddling with my playlist or thinking about the text messages and emails just on the other side of Do Not Disturb. None of that is what I need when working out.

Since switching over to my Ironman, I’ve found myself not even having my phone on me during a workout. I’ve gone full Luddite—something I always laughed at in the gym but now have huge respect for. Everything becomes harder without a workout playlist, but it also becomes more zen. On a run, you listen to your feet on the pavement. During a lift, you listen to your breathing. You get a lot more in tune with your body.

This, however, is the real crux of my argument. The little smartwatch tracking rings don’t do it for me, and nothing pisses me off more than a notification badge. It’s a nerd with a stat sheet saying, “Well, if you observe last night’s sleep score and this morning’s use of caffeine, you’ll see it’s a great time to get in the gym,” while I’m sitting at the beach reading a book on vacation. I don’t want to be told when to work out or how hard to workout by someone’s algorithm.

What I want is something that sparks my internal motivation. For that, I keep the Ironman where I’ll have to see it every single day. At home, it’s on top of my phone when I wake up in the morning—that’s one time when I can hit the park for a few pull-ups or knock out 15 minutes of pushups in the kitchen. If I miss that, it has to go in my work bag, so that when I drop my laptop in, I see it and make a decision whether or not to hit the gym on the way home. When I travel, it’s on the hotel nightstand.

Reader, let me tell you how much sweeter it feels when you know it was your willpower, not a notification, that got you to move around. I’ve done midnight sprints in a hotel gym, 6 a.m. jump rope on the sidewalk, 6 p.m. squats in the gym. There are times where I go too hard or too light, but I’m learning to listen to my body—not an app—for cues on when it’s ready, and if I skip a workout for drinks with friends or a movie with my wife, I don’t beat myself up. It’s a little part of my brain, my soul that I haven’t given over to the tech overlords. All I needed to get in shape was a good watch.

#6874. The Transformative Experience of Buying Japanese Denim in Japan

I was never a big jeans guy. After a recent trip to Tokyo, I think I’m changed forever.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

A ponytailed man in head-to-toe denim orbits me a third, then a fourth time in quick succession, like his eyes are twin asteroids and my waist a powerful gravitational force. When he stops, suddenly, he calls out, “Thirty-two!” and asks, “Button or zipper?”

I should’ve expected this sort of wizardry when I entered Momotaro Jeans Aoyama, a Tokyo outlet of the highly esteemed Japanese denim purveyor. Though it’s housed on the second floor of a mall just a three-quarter-mile walk from overstimulating, people-packed Shibuya, this tiny shop feels tranquil, purposeful, reverent. Dark indigo pants are folded and piled neatly in wooden cubbies along the walls; thick jackets hang gently from floating racks. Such a scene differs greatly from my earliest jeans-shopping memory.

In my early teens, on an Easter Sunday trip into New York, my mother shooed me into the Times Square Levi’s to buy my first big-boy pair. I never wore jeans. I attended Catholic school for much of my youth, so most mornings I was stuffed into stiff, ugly khakis. In rebellion against forced formality, I’d sport sweatpants or basketball shorts outside school hours.

Naturally, this appalled my fashionable mother, who couldn’t have lived with herself if she dropped her teenage son off at coed social outings wearing swishy Adidas track pants. But why was her remedy to hit a retail store in the busiest neighborhood in America on a holiday weekend? Trying on fur coats in hell would be comparably pleasant.

We dodged shoppers yanking slim cuts off floor displays and dug through unkempt mounds of unfolded denim until Mom found a few sizes in a few washes and forced me into the back changing room to try ’em all on. Of course, I huffed and puffed. Of course, I whinged and complained. But I left with a pair of medium-wash straight legs that at least wouldn’t look embarrassing on a first date.

In the nearly two decades since, as my interest in menswear has graduated from begrudging to professional, mass-market clothing has plunged in quality but spiked in price. Consumers are faced with a harsh reality when buying new: Either replace your decaying polyester-blend garments every few years or buy pricier, sturdier stuff from reputable manufacturers. I diverted toward the latter path a few years ago when I purchased a $150 heavyweight cotton hoodie from American Giant. (Though that may not seem like much to an Esquire reader, it was quite the jump for a J. Crew Factory sales-rack goblin like me.)

I learned of raw, selvedge denim a year later, when a buddy who’s into menswear sent me a Reddit post depicting a pair of jeans standing up all on their own. This was the beauty of raw Japanese denim, my friend told me, which is manufactured in the spirit of heritage American workwear from a time when durability was paramount. Heavyweight. Premium dye. Selvedge, meaning it’s woven on old-school looms from high-quality cotton. (You can also buy cheaper, significantly less impressive Japanese denim, but we’re talking about the reputable specialists here.) I was intrigued until he mentioned how many hundreds of dollars they cost thanks to import fees and general U.S. financial fuckery. But if I were ever on a trip to Japan at a time when the U.S. dollar was stronger than the yen? Maybe I could swing a pair.

In September, I was invited on a work trip to Tokyo, and finally my chance presented itself. When I landed at Narita International Airport, all I could think about was trying on stiff, uncomfortable jeans—talk about a different mindset from that of teenage me in Times Square.

After a week of tight itineraries, I finally sneaked away on my very last day and made my way past the stanchions lining the perimeter of the Momotaro shop. I read online that some folks wait up to 30 minutes to enter; luckily, there is no queue when I arrive.

A handful of employees stand silently behind a broad counter, one of them the man with the ponytail, who approaches me when he sees me checking out the brand’s #100 Standard Straight jeans in 14.7-ounce denim, which is a measurement of weight per square yard of fabric. Instead of asking for my size, he does the circling bit described earlier. I reply to his follow-up question with “zipper,” and he shows me to a narrow changing room toward the back of the space and asks me to remove my shoes. Japanese etiquette. He arrives with two pairs of the very jeans I was checking out. I try them both on, but they don’t feel quite right.

“Anything thicker?” I ask, trying to overcome the language barrier between us. My Japanese is rudimentary; his English is solid in bursts but not completely fluent.

He returns with a straight-cut pair from Momotaro’s classic line that’s 15.7 ounces, but it has a button fly, which I struggle mightily to close. Alas, there are no zippered pairs of this kind left. Yet, of the three, these fit the best around my waist. I’m puzzled until I look closely at the tags. These classic button flies are a 32, but when I return to the standard zippers, I notice he has accidentally brought me a 31—way, way too tight in the crotch—and a 33, a hair too loose.

His eyes widen apologetically when I point out the error, and he rushes back to the stocks to ensure I get a 32 in my hands. They fit absolutely perfectly. Done and done. He’s relieved.

And just like that, I’m ready to walk out a satisfied customer. This is how I’m used to buying jeans, after all. You try ’em, you like ’em, you buy ’em, and if you get home and they’re not quite what you thought they were, you return ’em. But as I will soon learn, there’s more to it at Momotaro. My guy has me put them back on and step out once more.

“Baggy or not baggy?” he asks of how I’d like my jeans to appear.

In front of a mirror, he squats down and folds the bottoms of the legs up to just about my ankle line and slides a transparent clip over the surface to mark its place. An in-house tailor will hem them for me. I change back into my other clothes, and he explains as best he can how to properly take care of my jeans. I shouldn’t wash them too often, but when I do, I should fasten everything and turn them inside out. Oh, and keep them separate from other clothes, because the indigo will definitely run. That’s part of the fun; the jeans are designed to fade handsomely over time based on how and when I wear them. A personalized pair, if you will. Don’t ever dry them in a machine, though. Take ’em out, smooth the creases, and hang to dry in a cool, dark place.

He hands me a little box with a lifetime-guarantee card and explains that if I’m ever back in Japan and need a repair, they’ve got me covered.

A second employee guides me across the hall to a second storefront, where payment and hemming take place. After I show my passport to receive the 10 percent tax reduction for tourists, I swipe my card. $211. That’s it?! By shopping here, I essentially chop a third off the price listed on the Momotaro U.S. website. I’m told to return in 15 or so minutes once the hemming is done, so I dash to a café downstairs for a matcha over which I reflect on my experience.

Whenever I buy anything over a Benjamin, I immediately get buyer’s remorse. That American Giant hoodie? I spent a good few days lamenting that the sleeves were slightly too long before I talked myself out of a return. And yet, when I pick up my Momotaro jeans once they’re ready—they’re bagged neatly and wrapped in plastic to protect them from the day’s ceaseless rain—I feel beyond cared for. When I finally return home and put them on, I don’t fear that I maybe should have purchased a smaller size in case they stretch—they’re not supposed to—because my jeans fit perfectly. They’re right in proportion with my body, sit higher than I’m used to but quite comfortably, and style well with a graphic tee and light jacket.

Maybe I’m shopping at the wrong stores at home. Maybe I need to raise my price floor to get that kind of service. Or maybe we’ve lost something here in the U.S. that’s now attainable only overseas. Maybe I need to get back on a plane to Tokyo ASAP and hit all the stores I missed—Hinoya, Japan Blue, the Flat Head—then take the shinkansen to Osaka and get myself some really thick boys from Iron Heart, where 25-ounce denim is the gold standard.

Or maybe I should just enjoy the jeans I have now, wear them in nicely over the next decade until they’re washed to sky blue and softer than a lamb’s ear. I can look forward to passing them down to whatever kids I have one day. It’ll save us a trip to the Times Square Levi’s.

#6872. Your Horoscope for the Week of November 15

Last week, Mercury Retrograde and Venus Retrograde finally ended. This week, you get a moment to catch your breath as Scorpio season draws to a close (it'll end next Sunday, November 22). Along with enjoying the remainder of the Scorpio vibes while they last, there are two smaller cosmic events that you might notice this week.

First, Jupiter and Neptune Retrograde form a harmonious biquintile aspect on Sunday. In English, this means that the two planets are connecting in such a way that their energies are working together. You might notice a little extra luck or motivation today. Then, on Tuesday, the First Quarter Moon in Aquarius continues the "get to work" message. A First Quarter Moon marks the halfway point between a New Moon and a Full Moon, and it's a reminder to stay focused on your goals.

Are your spending habits in line with your ideals? In other words, are you putting your money where your mouth is?

On Sunday, the Jupiter-Neptune aspect helps you bring up an important subject with whoever that person is—whether a romantic partner, best friend, family member, or creative collaborator. #6871. This Puget Sound Home Kept All of Its Original Charm—While Hiding Every Modern Convenience

The design team faithfully restored the details that have the most heart.

When this Puget Sound home was built in 1908, its original owners gave it a name: Heart’s Delight. Years later, after the house was bought by another family, principal designer Lisa Staton, alongside her senior designer Lauren Galeotti, took on the task of restoring the space with that same sentiment in mind.

“My favorite thing about this house is all of the love it holds,” Galeotti says. The new owners became deeply attached to the space, and the neighbors had long adored the house, too. As work progressed, the contractors and tradespeople fell for it as well. “She really charmed us all, from start to finish,” Galeotti says of the home.

That affection guided a renovation rooted in respect for what was already there. “Our passion for heritage homes is fueled by the original craftsmanship, history, and charm the homes hold,” Staton explains.

Every piece of original millwork stayed, and any new elements added had to take their cues from the old—even if that meant undoing some of the design missteps of the past. For example, a custom, made-to-match fireplace now sits over the original brick-and-plaster hearth that had been painted white. Though it was a whole team effort to get right, it now flawlessly complements the living room’s woodwork.

The current homeowners are also avid entertainers, so the kitchen needed a total strategic (and stylistic) rework that wouldn’t expand beyond its footprint. Adding the backsplash tile, created with Dorset Delft artist Aviva Halter, and a textural floor tile were key to giving the room its inimitable character. Other splurges, like all-new lighting throughout and the powder room's striking mural wallpaper, were necessary investments in the home's future.

But the biggest one, Staton says, was restoring all original details that everyone had fallen in love with: "always worth the investment!"

FAST FACTSDesigner: Lisa Staton and Lauren Galeotti of Lisa Staton Interior DesignStylist: Cozbi Jean Location: Puget Sound, WashingtonThe Space: A four-bedroom, four-bathroom Craftsman-style home built in 1908

This living room already “glows with daylight” thanks to its original windows, and the millwork has charm and texture in spades, so the main task in updating this room was to create a cozy, after-hours vibe.

“We wanted to make it warm and inviting, and every surface and corner needed to be enveloping,” Staton says. She achieved this by mixing newly sourced finds with well-loved pieces from the family’s personal collection, and it all blends seamlessly.

After entering the home through the original French doors, guests are greeted by the great room. This entertaining-ready space is faithful to its original style with two very notable additions: the custom fireplace cladding and accordion panels directly above that open to reveal the TV. It’s a room that can easily turn a quick drop-by into an entire evening in. “A pair of matching sofas invite you to sit and chat for a while,” Staton adds.

Keeping the spirit of this kitchen intact while gut-renovating it was a challenge, but the trick was to hide away the modern conveniences wherever possible and draw the eye toward period-appropriate accents instead.

“In heritage homes, it is really helpful to bring in antique elements to the space to add warmth and character in imperfect items,” Galeotti says. In this case, the flush mount ceiling lights, stools, window treatments, and hutch bring in texture and patina that add to the room’s authenticity.

Combining the storage and function of a kitchen island with the rustic character of a cook’s table, the kitchen’s central surface feels more like a piece of furniture than an extension of the cabinetry. “Since we did not need to put any appliances in the island, that really became an opportunity to keep beauty at the forefront,” Galeotti explains.

One of the team’s favorite parts of the kitchen, though, lies in the smaller details: the handmade backsplash tiles, which bring a personal, playful touch. “The client chose the subject matter of each custom-painted tile, including meaningful flowers and even their pets!” Galeotti says. Their Dorset Delft style makes the stoneware feel like it has always belonged in the house.

Though space is certainly tight in this pantry, it’s a workhorse area in its own right. “We designed custom cabinetry to line the walls with plenty of storage, and were even able to squeeze in a wall oven, microwave, and warming drawer,” Galeotti notes. This discreet appliance placement also helps the main cook space retain its ageless atmosphere.

The homeowners asked for this newly added powder room to feel “whimsical,” and Staton’s team granted that wish, selecting a dusky Andrew Martin mural wallcovering that depicts a landscape with traditional folk art motifs. Its towering pear trees, mythical creatures, and rambling vales read like an epic fairy tale across the four walls. “It is a small room, but we wanted to make a big impact,” she says.

According to Galeotti, “the dining room lives in the same footprint and largely looks exactly like the day it was built!” The team restored the original built-in cabinet, whose diamond-patterned panes echo the room’s leaded-glass windows. This clean-line geometry contrasts beautifully with the curves, plumes, and ruffled silhouettes seen in the window treatments and Murano glass chandelier.

Updating the primary bedroom’s layout was another necessary correction to previous design choices—the team took down a wall and turned two rooms into one. “However, given how the windows are on the large wall where the rooms used to be divided, it may have originally been one space,” Galeotti adds. Now, there’s an unobstructed view out to the water and mountains just beyond the property. They also added a his-and-hers closet, new flooring, and a bed Galeotti describes as a “perfect cloud” from which to greet the surrounding scenery each day.

Previously, the primary bathroom was completely open to the bedroom, with carpeting and a step up to access it. “It was a bit of a strange situation,” Staton admits. A serious layout rework was in order so that the space is now separate, level with the bedroom, and equipped with a freestanding tub and beautifully tiled shower. “We also added a feature we do in most bathrooms: use vintage or antique mirrors as doors to create medicine cabinets,” she adds.

“The addition of this bedroom at the back of the house gives guests a really private retreat in a quiet corner,” Galeotti says. With its bobbin bed frame, antique furniture finds, and misty color palette, this sleep space provides a soft place to land.

“This is the kids’ bathroom, but we imagined a bathroom that felt beautiful for children, guests, and grown kids alike,” Galeotti notes. The space originally suffered from the same “watch the step” quirk that the main bathroom did, so Staton installed a similar solution here: She put the plumbing into the floor and incorporated all-new finishes and fixtures to add elegant polish that all ages can appreciate.

Lisa Staton Interior Design is a boutique firm established in 2004 with roots in the East Coast and Seattle, united by a shared love for enhancing older homes and creating new ones inspired by historic craftsmanship. The team brings a unique blend of both modern and traditional elements to a wide array of homes.

#6870. Actually, Robert Pattinson Would Make a Perfect James Bond

My colleagues and editors are sick of hearing me say it, but I believe it with my whole chest: The actor playing James Bond should never be more famous than James Bond. There’s something about Bond—his cultural footprint and the dangerous yet seductive life he leads in his stories—that makes him too big to be a footnote in a great actor’s résumé. James Bond creates leading men; James Bond is not for leading men to create.

So you have to know that it’s not easy for me to say this: Robert Pattinson would be fantastic and exactly the new James Bond we’re looking for.

You’ve probably heard by now that Amazon, which acquired full creative control over all things Bond from longtime owners Eon Productions and the Broccoli family, has been searching for the next 007 in a movie to be directed by Denis Villeneuve.

What was once an arena of speculation has exploded into a circus, with different names being bandied about at once—namely Callum Turner, Josh O’Connor, and Jack Lowden. (There is obviously a specific type of white man they’re going for here.) Just when it seemed as if the new Bond would finally be unveiled, there came a rush of two really big names suddenly in the running. Among them is Pattinson, reported by The Hollywood Reporter on November 6, along with Nicholas Hoult. Pattinson secretly auditioned for the role during a break in filming for The Batman: Part II, which halted filming in October when Matt Reeves attended to a personal matter.

No shade to Hoult or any of the other actors, but Pattinson is now the most interesting candidate despite a whiff of overexposure this year. Between The Drama in the spring, The Odyssey in the summer, Primetime in the fall, and soon Dune: Part Three in the dead of winter, 2026 has been shaping up into a banner year for Pattinson. Not since Twilight has the man been so prolific on our screens.

But even before the year began, Pattinson had long ago outgrown his image of a tween heartthrob. He’s a prodigious talent capable of daring choices and executing on them in a way you can’t ever expect. (Who else would say cock like this?) His popular line from The Odyssey—“Somebody get these beggars out of here!”—didn’t become a meme on its own. Pattinson made beggars sound like a slur, and in the same breath, his nervous blinking and quivering smile cemented Antinous as an unconfident weasel whose puffed-out chest was, in fact, filled with hot air. Unlike previous Batman actors, who often played the Caped Crusader like a knight in matte black armor, Pattinson’s interpretation was/is that of an antisocial weirdo everyone stares at like a freak. Which he is.

Like Batman, Bond has also been a role that people misunderstand, sometimes even by the men who play him. (It is not my biases talking here; I really do think Daniel Craig is the only actor to ever nail the nuances and pathology of the character.) Bond is stylish and can say the right thing at the right time, be it to enemies or lovers. But he’s not a dashing or noble hero. He’s a brute in Brunello Cucinelli. A thug with a $9,000 Omega. He literally kills for a living—and had to kill to get that right—and his attachment to his job is downright destabilizing.

Recall in Skyfall, after Judi Dench’s M orders Moneypenny to shoot, Bond copes with endless drinking, womanizing, and gambling. That might sound like a fun weekend in Vegas, but in the context of Bond, his fallout from discovering his expendability led him to a really, really dark path. It is not normal to drink shots with a scorpion on your hand, nor is it healthy to be up in the morning reaching for booze from behind the bar. Bond might live a life many of us men dream of, but it comes at a severe cost. I don’t know what kind of Bond movie Villeneuve has in mind, but if the creative team is lining up like this, we’re in for something special.

You can’t exactly guesstimate what kind of 007 Pattinson might be based on his previous roles. There is truly little common ground between Batman and Bond. But one thing is for sure: If Pattinson gets the part, we are in for a goddamn show.

#6864. 34 Unique Christmas Tree Themes That'll Inspire You This Holiday Season

From bejeweled ornaments to popcorn garlands, these ideas bring major holiday cheer.

When it comes to Christmas tree decor, some folks keep it classic, using the same ornaments every year, while others choose new themes. Both approaches capture the charm and spirit of the season. However, if you find yourself in the latter cohort, you know how hard it can be to find yearly inspiration that will wow your guests and blend with your existing decor. So, if you’re in need of a muse, we’ve curated a list of tree themes so stunning, you’ll want to repeat them next year.

While many people stick to the traditional charm of classic hues, this year offers bold alternatives. From glittering jeweled ornaments to vintage-inspired touches like tinsel, popcorn garlands, and flocked branches, social feeds are buzzing with fresh, trendy ideas and new possibilities. There's a lot of Christmas creativity to spread the cheer this year.

To spark your imagination, we've compiled 34 merry and bright Christmas tree themes that are sure to bring jolly spirits. Whether you're drawn to the rustic farmhouse aesthetic or punchy plum color trends, these seasonal styles will infuse your home with magic and joy—and as a bonus, they look fantastic on both real and faux firs.

A Christmas tree can quickly become crowded, so take inspiration from Anthropologie and Terrain's holiday house and swap your standard ornaments for oversized baubles. These larger, multicolored orbs make a true statement, especially when paired with vibrant lights or flocked branches.

Enjoying a warm drink after a long day of skiing was the inspiration for this miniature tree. Featuring gloves, boots, and a Santa with his own pair of skis, take inspiration and decorate a tree dedicated to your favorite seasonal hobby.

For those who like their ornaments to be matchy-matchy, it might be time to start collecting. Anthropologie displayed an adorable miniature tree decked out with festive mice. It's accompanied by a village fit for their Christmas-ready friends.

Ideal for the girly-girl in your life, or maybe even a coastal holiday setting, think outside of the box and abandon the evergreen altogether this year. Instead, take inspiration from design firm BEFoundCollective's white Christmas tree, decorated with cheerful pastel ornaments.

There are few things cozier than a flocked Christmas tree. This one by Pottery Barn is adorned with cozy and metallic ornaments like stars, birds, and even pinecones. This Christmas tree theme is great for a mountain or woodsy cabin, but you can even bring that countryside Christmas energy into your city apartment.

A soothing yet vibrant shade, you can't go wrong with a Christmas tree decked out in peach and orange tones. The silver tinsel contrasts perfectly while adding some glitz and glam. This tree, by the design firm BEFoundCollective in Virginia, showcases vintage ornaments among the lush foliage.

Interior designer Beata Heuman ties dozens of bows on the branches of her showroom's Christmas tree for easy and low-cost DIY decorations. Use ribbons in the traditional Christmas color scheme, match your interior's decor, or, like Heuman, choose hues from the entire rainbow.

Perfect for warmer climates (or those wishing spring would circle back around already), forgo traditional ornaments and adorn your tree with faux florals. Take inspiration from the Winterthur Museum, Garden, and Library in Delaware and use more voluminous blooms like hydrangeas as filler between smaller flowers like mums or daffodils.

A classic color scheme beloved by modern, vintage, and even Art Deco lovers, a black and gold palette is timeless. Make your tree stand tall with a bold trunk cover and top with a snowflake-inspired star to tie your entire decor scheme together.

Topped with a cheerful snowman, this Christmas tree theme is all about the seasonal icons we've come to love. Gnomes and mini Santas stand out against the lush foliage of the tree while striped baubles and twinkling stars glam up the traditional red-and-white color palette.

If your little one is still running around singing "Part of Your World" from The Little Mermaid, pay tribute to their favorite princess with a sophisticated Christmas tree theme. Chock-full of glittering mermaids, chic metal whales, and glossy sea turtles, this tree designed by Pottery Barn is the perfect way to bring the film to life for the holidays.

While winter whites always get the bulk of the attention, blue is the other color of the season we're suggesting you embrace. Opt for chinoiserie-style ornaments and a decadent velvet ribbon garland to make it feel just as cozy as red and green.

Perfect for the diva of the family, this Anthropologie-designed Christmas tree is all about glamorous, textured accents like peacock feathers, Depression-style glass, and velvet accents. Christmas is allowed to be over-the-top!

The summer of Barbie may be over, but that joyous love of all things pink can (and should) continue all year long. If you're feeling as bold as Margot Robbie's iconic character, go all in on a bright pink tree with gold and white accents. Like Barbie, your Christmas tree can be anything you want it to.

If you're always looking at moon phases and tarot cards, bring that love of all things astrological to your Christmas decorations this year. Golden crescent moons, flying doves, and iron-star-studded wreaths are incorporated in this flocked tree. Add warm-hued string lights, and the decorations take on an ethereal glow.

Classics are classics for a reason—and nothing is more instantly festive than a hallmark red-and-white Christmas tree. This bountiful beauty was decked by Inspired by Charm, who included lodge-inspired elements like buffalo check wrapping paper and wooden skis to make it feel fresh and fanciful.

If a streamlined and sleek aesthetic is more your style, use this Christmas tree by For the Home as inspiration. The intentionally sparse look nods to traditional Scandinavian spruces, which feature less dense branches and are left mostly bare save for a few basic ball ornaments and twinkling white lights.

Why choose just two colors when you can have all of them? This Technicolor tree created by Serretta Style leans into a more-is-more aesthetic with tiers of ornaments in every color of the rainbow. While decorations in the same shapes and designs are used here (just in different shades), you could get the same effect by hanging your existing ornaments by color from top to bottom.

The weather outside may be frightful, but it certainly inspires a delightful Christmas tree design! Nod to all things snow and ice with a winter-wonderland-themed evergreen. This scene designed by Lolly Jane combines a flocked tree with an assortment of white and silver ornaments—like plush "snowballs" and frosty pinecones—for a snowy and chic scene. Plenty of warm white lights and a pair of ice skates complete the charming look.

On top of being the magazine's go-to Waco expert, Meghan also touches on quite a few other verticals in the digital space, like hot news content featuring your favorite HGTV stars, home design inspiration, and the latest interior TikTok trends. However, her favorite pieces are the ones that start conversations, such as stories about resale value and whether or not the Solo cup is chic. Before becoming a home writer, Meghan worked in the fashion industry for two years, writing for Harper's Bazaar after graduating from Iowa State University with a bachelor's degree in apparel merchandising. When she's not interviewing interior designers about home trends, you can find Meghan reading a fictional thriller with her cat in her lap, planning themed parties, or strolling through flea markets searching for chunky candle holders and vintage bookends.

#6862. Absolutely Everything You Need to Know About the Citizen Watch Catalog

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Japanese brand offers endless variety, appealing affordability, and incredible technological innovation.

Whether you encounter them in multi-brand boutiques and stand-alone stores, in duty-free shops while on vacation, on the Internet, or at auction, Citizen’s watches are everywhere.

Founded in 1918, the brand that began with the simple objective of producing a Japanese-made watch has evolved into a truly global juggernaut, with annual turnover of approximately $2.1 billion for the fiscal year that ended in March 2026. Vertically integrated, it produces everything from mechanical divers of the traditional ilk to tech-forward, satellite-guided modern tool watches that update time zones instantly as one zigzags across the world. Its light-powered Eco-Drive technology essentially constitutes a movement category in its own right, while its wafer-thin Eco-Drive One models—some of the brand’s highest-end wares—command luxury prices.

Like Seiko—another homegrown Japanese brand—Citizen’s considerable scale and power has compounded over more than a century. And also like Seiko, it has grown its manufacturing capabilities to such an extent that it has successfully expanded into industries other than watchmaking. But where Citizen truly shines is in offering a remarkably capable and robust product at a price that elicits few complaints from the watch-buying public. Available in points of sale in roughly 130 countries—500 alone in the U.S.—Citizen’s timepieces are not only affordable but also truly ubiquitous.

For someone in search of a relatively inexpensive watch—any type of watch, really, save perhaps a high complication in precious metal with a dial made by a lone monk living in the Swiss Jura—that will hold up under adverse conditions, Citizen is a natural answer. Whether you’re looking for a feature-laden dive watch (Promaster Aqualand), an elegant, ultrathin dress watch (Eco-Drive One), a luxurious, design-forward everyday watch (Series 8), or a vintage-inspired take on a Genta-esque design (Zenshin), there’s a good chance that there’s a Citizen timepiece that will satisfy your desires.

The only issue is parsing the firm’s gigantic catalog in search of an ideal choice. To help you in this regard, we produced this handy guide.

While not as old as Seiko, Citizen has a long and storied watchmaking history filled with a staggering amount of technological innovation. In fact, it wouldn’t be too bold to claim that in a world based primarily on centuries-old technology, Citizen is one of the most forward-thinking marques.

The brand’s story begins in 1918, when entrepreneur Kamekichi Yamazaki established the Shokosha Watch Research Institute in Tokyo with the goal of providing a domestically produced alternative to imported Swiss and American watches. By 1924, it had produced its first pocket watch, which it named “Citizen” at the behest of Tokyo mayor Count Shinpei Goto, who imagined a watch available to ordinary members of society. Six years later, the Shokosha Watch Research Institute was officially incorporated as the Citizen Watch Company and began producing wristwatches in 1931.

In 1936, the company established its Tanashi factory in Tokyo, allowing it to significantly expand its industrial capacity and begin exporting watches to Southeast Asia, China, and elsewhere. By 1941, it established a machine-tool business that allowed it to move beyond watchmaking and that still exists to this day. Postwar expansion continued at a dizzying rate, with Citizen developing shock-resistant Parashock technology in 1956 (which it demonstrated by dropping watches out of a helicopter). In 1959, it debuted the Parawater, Japan’s first fully waterproof watch, and in 1962 came the Diamond Flake, an ultrathin model equipped with a hand-wound movement.

By the 1960s, Citizen began experimenting with technologies that would come to define its place in the contemporary watch market: First, in 1966, came the X-8, the country’s first electronic wristwatch, followed in 1970 by the X-8 Chronometer, which holds the distinction of being the world’s very first titanium wristwatch. And while the brand wasn’t the first to embrace quartz technology, it did so with vigor by 1973, introducing the Crystron Mega in 1975, which was accurate to an astonishing ±3 seconds per year.

The technology that would eventually evolve into Eco-Drive—the brand’s signature system that converts ambient light into electrical energy, thus reducing the frequency of battery replacements—debuted in 1976 with the advent of its Crystron Solar Cell, the world’s first light-powered, analog-dial quartz watch. Then, in 1982, Citizen released the Professional Diver, which boasted an incredible-for-the-era 1,300 meters of water resistance; it followed up three years later with the Aqualand, the first dive watch with an onboard electronic depth gauge. In 1989, it gathered its diverse sports watch offerings together within the Promaster line.

Innovation continued throughout the 1990s, with Citizen introducing the world’s first multi-zone radio-controlled wristwatch in 1993. In 2011, it debuted the Eco-Drive Satellite Wave, a watch that synchronized with satellites to provide hyper-accurate timekeeping across different time zones. Five years later, in 2016, the Eco-Drive One hit the market with a light-powered movement just 1mm thick, building upon ultrathin offerings from the brand’s back catalog. Then, in 2019, it brought forth the Eco-Drive Caliber 0100, a movement with stunning accuracy of ±1 second per year.

Today, Citizen Group is more than just Citizen, comprising movement maker Miyota, the aforementioned precision machinery business, watch brands Bulova and Frédérique Constant, and much more. It’s a vertically integrated behemoth with more than 12,000 employees worldwide. Founded over a century ago, it’s grown into one of the most important brands within the watchmaking sphere—and beyond.

Like fellow Japanese watchmaker Seiko, Citizen produces such a dizzying array of timepieces that it’s nearly impossible to categorize them in any definitive manner. Many modern collections overlap in remit with older historical collections that are still kicking around, while certain collections are available only in certain markets. (Some frankly defy categorization entirely, and others should probably be cut from the brand’s catalog.)

What follows, therefore, is a helpful (albeit admittedly non-exhaustive) list of Citizen’s most important and recognizable product families, delineated with the understanding that certain watches—including several dedicated ladies collections from the Japanese domestic market—will invariably be left out. Still, the following rough descriptions should help orient the new (or seasoned!) collector.

The Rolan Collection is an excellent place to search for a highly affordable, versatile everyday watch. Using Citizen’s signature Eco-Drive technology, each model is easily powered by ambient light and virtually never requires a battery change. While certain references are paired to matching metal bracelets, others come on stylish straps, and all take their aesthetic inspiration from classic mid-century horological tropes. Simple and handsome, the Rolan Collection watches are perfect for someone in search of their first “nice” watch.

The ref. AW2110-06E is everything you need in a dress/everyday watch and nothing you don’t. Measuring 41mm in brushed stainless steel, it features a radially brushed black sector dial with applied indices and a luminous dauphine handset. Paired to a black leather strap with stainless-steel hardware and powered by Eco-Drive technology, it’s a timepiece that could conceivably be paired with just about anything. Moreover, at just a hair over $300, it makes a highly affordable gift for a recent graduate who needs a solid timepiece to wear on an interview or to a first job.

Notable Reference: AW2110-06EType: DressDiameter: 41mmMovement: Citizen cal. J830 Eco-DrivePrice: $296.25MORE INFO HERE

The Garrison Collection contains military-inspired field watches and chronographs in a variety of styles, most of which are widely available in the United States both online and at authorized dealers. Powered by Eco-Drive technology, they’re reliable, good-looking pieces on bracelets or straps that all retail for less than $500, making them—like their counterparts in the dressier Rolan collection—excellent gifting options. For someone who’s after mil-spec looks but who doesn’t want to shell out for a vintage watch, the Garrison is a perfect choice.

The ref. BM8180-03E is ubiquitous at Citizen points of sale and neatly encapsulates the brand’s ethos. Measuring a comfortable 37mm in brushed stainless steel, it features a black dial with luminous Arabic indices, an outer five-minute track, and a day-date display at three o’clock. Powered by Eco-Drive, highly legible, and wildly affordable, it still boasts 100 meters of water resistance and a two-piece green fabric strap for less than $300. Looking like an issued military watch from the 1960s, it’s ideal for outdoor use while hiking or on vacation

Notable Reference: BM8180-03EType: FieldDiameter: 37mmMovement: Citizen cal. E100 Eco-DrivePrice: $221.25MORE INFO HERE

Established in 1989, the Promaster Collection is where Citizen parks its most formidable tool watches. Organized into Marine, Sky, and Land subcategories, they localize many of the notable innovations that the brand developed over the past 60-plus years, from the early Parawater’s water resistance to the Aqualand’s electronic depth gauge to the Navihawk’s multifunction timekeeping and more. Today the collection encompasses everything from mechanical divers to radio-controlled pilot’s watches to exploration-oriented field watches.

Though Citizen is famous for its Eco-Drive technology, it’s worth noting that the brand has a long history of crafting hardcore mechanical tool watches. The modern Promaster Fujitsubo ref. NB6026-56L—a limited edition based upon a vintage 1977 diver found still ticking on an Australian beach in 1983—is one such timepiece. Crafted from Super Titanium with a platinum DLC coating, it features a marine-blue bezel and dial, a matching triple-link bracelet, 200 meters of water resistance, and an automatic movement with a power reserve of 42 hours.

Notable Reference: NB6026-56LType: DiverDiameter: 41mmMovement: Citizen cal. 9051 automaticPrice: $1,195MORE INFO HERE

Launched in 2008, discontinued in 2014, and relaunched in 2021, the Series 8 Collection is based upon the idea of an angular, eight-part case design containing a mechanical movement. Though the collection has expanded somewhat beyond its original aesthetic remit, it still emphasizes the idea of a contemporary mechanical offering with clean lines and bold geometric forms. Comprising several model families, the Series 8 features integrated-style bracelets, textured dials, and enhanced resistance to magnetic fields of up to 16,000 A/m.

Executed in a tasteful two-tone colorway, the Series 8 831 ref. NB6059-57P offers outsize value from a variety of angles. There’s its 40mm stainless-steel case with surprisingly sophisticated geometry. But there’s also its handsome Champagne dial with rose-gold-tone furniture, its caliber 9051 automatic movement, its tapered H-link bracelet with brushed and finished surfaces, and its 100 meters of water resistance. For someone looking for a handsome and versatile everyday watch that can be worn to the office or to the beach, it’s an excellent, relatively affordable option.

Notable Reference: NB6059-57PType: Everyday Diameter: 40mmMovement: Citizen cal. 9051 automaticPrice: $1,125MORE INFO HERE

Dating to 1987, the Attessa Collection focuses on the marriage of advanced materials with technologies such as Eco-Drive, radio control, and GPS timekeeping. Building upon Citizen’s innovations with titanium from 1970’s X-8 Chronometer, it offers lightweight, function-forward watches in a variety of categories. Sporty, practical, and good-looking, the collection’s tool watches ditch classically inspired looks in favor of aesthetics that balance practical functionality with sleek lines, subdued materials, and elaborate case finishing.

The Attesa F950 ref. CC4107-80H encapsulates many of the technologies that make Citizen’s wares stand out in an extremely crowded marketplace. There’s the 43.2mm Super Titanium case in dark gray DLC with recrystallized titanium accents; the Eco-Drive Satellite Wave GPS movement with time-only signal reception in under three seconds; and the multifunction display with world time, chronograph, perpetual calendar, power reserve, and numerous other features. Fueled by exposure to ambient light, it’s a futuristic tool with streaks of ’70s inspiration.

Notable Reference: CC4107-80HType: Everyday Diameter: 43.2mmMovement: Citizen cal. F950 Eco-DrivePrice: $2,195MORE INFO HERE

While Citizen has several lines that focus on mechanical watchmaking, Tsuyosa is particularly notable for combining this with stainless-steel construction, integrated-bracelet styling, colorful dials, and accessible pricing. Rather than emphasizing advanced materials or modern timekeeping tech, it uses classic 20th-century watchmaking as an inspiration, seemingly adopting the idea that simpler is better. From pastel-hued 37mm models to small-seconds designs with undulating dial patterns, it’s one of the brand’s most compelling offerings.

The Tsuyosa 37mm is truly “everything you need and nothing you don’t” in wristwatch form. Constructed from stainless steel, it features alternating brushed and polished surfaces throughout its multifaceted case and integrated multi-link bracelet, lending it a sophistication that belies its sub-$1,000 pricing. Available in multiple colors, its sunray dial with luminous indices and magnified date window is legible and direct, while its automatic caliber 8210 movement and 50 meters of water resistance offer more than enough utility for daily wear. A set-and-forget watch.

Notable Reference: NJ0200-50MType: Everyday Diameter: 37mmMovement: Citizen cal. 8210 automaticPrice: $371.25MORE INFO HERE

The Zenshin Collection brings together several compelling features in a single product family: Super Titanium (titanium construction with Duratect surface-hardening treatments), automatic or Eco-Drive movements, angular cases with integrated bracelets inspired by Citizen’s 1980s output, textured dials, sapphire crystals, 100 meters of water resistance, and relatively accessible pricing. Available in three-hand and chronograph configurations, Zenshin brings a bit more materials science than Tsuyosa but costs much less than comparable Attesa fare.

Though a 42.4mm chronograph might smack of early-2000s excess, Super Titanium construction and a comfortable integrated bracelet help keep the Zenshin Chrono under control on the wrist. Available in several colorways, the “panda” colorway ref. CA4610-85A with a textured white-silver dial and contrasting black outer tachymeter scale and chronograph registers is particularly attractive and versatile. Powered by Eco-Drive, it features a 24-hour display as well as a date window—and its sub-$600 price is tough to argue with.

Notable Reference: CA4610-85AType: Everyday Diameter: 42.4mmMovement: Citizen cal. B620 Eco-DrivePrice: $506.25MORE INFO HERE

The Citizen is Citizen’s purest expression of horological excellence. Building upon the firm’s legacy of highly accurate mechanical timekeeping in the mid-century Chronomaster Collection, it launched the collection in 1995 to emphasize its commitment to precision, simplicity, and longevity. Today it comprises both mechanical and Eco-Drive movements, marrying them with traditional Japanese craftsmanship in the form of washi paper dials, hand-dyed indigo treatments, and other decorative techniques. Wildly accurate, they’re among Citizen’s most expensive offerings.

If you’re the type of person who values accuracy above all else in a wristwatch—but wouldn’t mind great looks thrown in for good measure—then chances are the Citizen ref. AQ4100-65X might be the timepiece for you. Measuring 38.3mm in the brand’s Super Titanium, it’s powered by the Eco-Drive caliber A060, which is accurate to ±5 seconds per year. (And that innocuous-seeming date window? It’s actually a perpetual date window that doesn’t need manual updating.) Oh, and the red dial? Ultra-thin Tosa Tengujoshi paper. (Hence the price.)

Notable Reference: AQ4100-65XType: Dress/everyday Diameter: 38.3mmMovement: Citizen cal. A060 Eco-DrivePrice: $2,995MORE INFO HERE

If the Citizen Collection is all about accuracy, then the Eco-Drive One Collection is about combining that accuracy—achieved via the brand’s signature Eco-Drive technology—with mind-bogglingly thin case architecture and precision manufacturing. Launched in 2016 to coincide with the 40th anniversary of Eco-Drive, the lineup features several watches powered by the caliber 8826, which was introduced as the world’s thinnest light-powered movement at just 1mm tall. When it’s combined with Super Titanium and other advanced tech, these pieces are truly remarkable.

Not only is the Eco-Drive One a spectacularly thin timepiece at just 4.5mm tall, but it’s also a remarkably handsome dress watch. Measuring 36.6mm wide in stainless steel, it features a flat gray dial with a contrasting dark-gray minute track, applied gold-tone indices with a matching dauphine handset, and a dark-gray sub-seconds display above six o’clock. That its crown is tiny shouldn’t be much of a concern—the caliber 8845 Eco-Drive is so accurate that you’ll hardly ever need to adjust it. (Unless you change time zones, that is.) Truly a one-and-done dress watch.

Notable Reference: AQ5010-01AType: DressDiameter: 36.6mmMovement: Citizen cal. 8845 Eco-DrivePrice: $2,395MORE INFO HERE

A limited-edition offering celebrating 50 years of Eco-Drive technology, the Eco-Drive Photon Collection combines highly geometric case shapes with dials inspired by the physics of light transmission. Currently available in two references, each features a slit dial that dynamically shifts in color as light interacts with its surface. Paired with integrated bracelets and measuring 39.6mm in Super Titanium, they’re more premium offerings than many Citizen core-catalog models but significantly less expensive than Eco-Drive One or Citizen watches.

A distinctive, contemporary take on the luxury sports watch, the Eco-Drive Photon ref. BJ6569-59X combines dark-gray and amber-yellow Super Titanium case construction to eye-catching effect. The three-dimensional dial with overlapping slits creates even more visual interest, while the caliber E036 Eco-Drive movement turns any light source into a consistent source of accurate timekeeping. Water resistant to 50 meters, the Photon is an excellent everyday option for someone who appreciates a bit of Genta-inflected styling in their wardrobe.

Notable Reference: BJ6569-59XType: EverydayDiameter: 39.6mmMovement: Citizen cal. E036 Eco-DrivePrice: $1,195MORE INFO HERE

#6860. Stop Hunting for Pricey Antiques—Designers Say These 9 Humble Finds Give a Home So Much Character

They’re affordable, widely available, and stylish.

It’s any avid thrifter’s dream to stumble upon an extremely valuable piece of decor while browsing the shelves of their favorite antique store. But while you’re hunting for rare treasures, don’t discount the less-expensive finds—they can be the secret to a home that looks instantly filled with character.

“To me, a collected home is much more interesting when everything isn’t perfect or chosen for its monetary value,” designer Lauren Sullivan of Well by Design tells House Beautiful. “Often, it’s the less precious pieces that give a room its personality.”

Even as older generations are continuing to downsize and offload unwanted things to local thrift stores, it still might be hard to find the diamonds in the rough. Keep reading to find out what Sullivan, and a handful of other designers, say you should be on the lookout for on your next thrifting visit.

The most valuable antiques are the ones that have been passed down through generations. In Sullivan’s own bathroom, the designer keeps her great-grandmother’s vanity set on the countertop, its aged finish providing beautiful contrast with the more contemporary marble and finishes. In her bedroom, the same great-grandmother’s small bedside clock found a place on Sullivan’s nightstand.

“Neither is particularly valuable monetarily, but I love incorporating pieces that have been passed down through generations into a home filled with both old and new,” Sullivan says.

Designers love incorporating artwork into homes to add personality and dimension, but Sullivan, as well as designer Becca Meyer of BB Meyer Design, believe that antique shops are where you can really find some hidden gems.

“Some of my favorite pieces of art have come from flea markets or antique shops for very little money,” Meyer says. “I much prefer these to mass produced prints.” You’re more likely to find pieces with aged textures at these types of shops, and that patinaed quality creates an entirely unique look.

Sullivan explains that she’s actually particularly drawn to prints, as well as smaller paintings and silhouettes. She even has an inexpensive print of The Skating Minister in a vintage frame in one of her guest rooms, pictured above. “These smaller, or sometimes quirky, pieces can bring so much personality to a space without requiring a significant investment,” she adds. They may not be originals, but they’re definitely unique.

Old pottery and ceramics are widely available, and they are usually affordable as well. Designer Brooke Snyder of Design Hutch loves how they make any place feel more curated.

The imperfections these handmade pieces carry, from uneven shapes, variations in glaze, or evidence of years of use, are what help these items bring a warmth to a room, particularly when placed near more refined materials, according to Sullivan. They feel humble, maybe even utilitarian, especially when displayed in a kitchen. Or, better yet, used in a kitchen (as long as they’re safe).

“They're beautiful on their own but also so functional for holding utensils or branches or flowers,” Meyer adds. “They make any space feel a little more lived-in and loved and help tell a story—and they don't break the bank!”

“I love incorporating vintage plates, old copper pots, and woven baskets into a home,” explains designer Joe Waroquier. Though these are run-of-the-mill, everyday objects, they can take on and provide an entirely different life when displayed in the home as decor. Mismatched plates on a wall, a collection of copper pots hanging on a rod or resting on shelves in the kitchen, an old crock filled with branches—these items invite in warmth, texture, and more personality.

“I’m particularly drawn to pieces that show their age, whether it’s a little patina, a worn finish, or an imperfect glaze,” he adds. “I love mixing these finds with more contemporary furnishings because that contrast makes a room feel collected and personal rather than overly designed.”

Mirrors can be intriguing focal points of a room, or they can add dimension to a gallery wall. However you want to style them, Snyder loves finding these reflective pieces at antique malls and vintage shops.

“Purchasing something that you don’t see every day from a retailer allows this unique piece to stand out on its own, without feeling like something you see everywhere or feels too simple,” she explains.

As Snyder puts it, antique table lamps provide high visual impact with low investment. Adding one to your accent table or on a bookshelf is an easy way to make your space feel more curated while inviting in another level of ambience with the lighting.

These vintage lamps in the above space, for instance, were bought from a collector and make the primary bedroom feel much more charming and curated. And unlike antique sconces, there’s no need to hardwire these into the wall.

Besides pottery and lamps, designer Ryann Swan Hackett of Ryann Swan Design loves to find and collect other small tabletop decor for her projects. Think candleholders, vintage coffee table books, and trays—things that can layer a little history and individuality into a room or simple vignette.

“If you’re lucky,” Swan Hackett says of the vintage coffee table books, “you’ll find an old inscription inside, which gives the book a story of its own.”

In terms of midsize accent pieces, Meyer says that she has a soft spot for finding antique stools “because you can pop them into any space to add depth and dimension.” Tuck them beside an armchair in the living room, add them to an empty kitchen corner and place a plant on top, or stick one in a bathroom by the tub for somewhere to keep a drink while relaxing. And if you find an upholstered one that could use new fabric, switching it out for a pattern you love is probably worth it.

“These bring a natural warmth that can be difficult to replicate with something new, and their small size makes them fairly reasonable in price for a vintage furnishing piece,” Meyer says.

A larger piece of furniture is likely always going to be more expensive than a smaller one, but designer Yena Jung of By Yena Designs thinks vintage hutches are the versatile antiques people should pay more attention to. They come in many different shapes, styles, and sizes, and can easily make a home in any room of the house.

“A beautiful vintage hutch can add warmth, charm, and a sense of history to a space,” Jung says. “I especially love how their imperfections and natural patina make them feel collected over time.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6859. Maura Higgins Reportedly Postponed ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Rehearsals Leading to Semifinals

“Mark had to readjust some of the choreo.”

Maura Higgins has been battling a rib injury behind the scenes on Dancing With the Stars, and the reality star has reportedly had to postpone multiple rehearsals this week leading to the semifinals.

“Mark had to readjust some of the choreo for her on next week’s dance,” a source tells The Sun. “They also had to postpone two different rehearsals so she can rest. But she’s pushing through the pain—which is getting worse every week but she’s determined to not let it stop her.”

Here’s Maura arriving to rehearsals on November 15, so clearly she’s still in it to win it.

Viewers saw Maura experience rib pain during rehearsals for Grammy Week, and she told Entertainment Tonight after the show that she’d ended up in the hospital. “It was quite a traumatic week. I’m not gonna lie,” she said. “Like we lost so much rehearsal time. I ended up in the hospital. I couldn’t really breathe. The swelling was so bad. But I didn’t want to give up. I didn’t want to throw the towel in. And I literally was like I just need to fight through this. I’m just glad I’m still here. And I’m feeling so much better now.”

Maura also confirmed that while X-rays showed there’s no crack in her rib, muscle and cartilage damage would “feel the exact same” and that the only treatment is to wait and let it heal.

Here’s to hoping Maura gets a chance to rest up and heal ahead of the DWTS semifinals Tuesday night!

#6858. So Is Lanterns Canceled Or What?

It was one of the best new HBO shows in recent memory, but it seems like that won’t matter at all.

You know what happens when you blow up a universe? You’re left sifting through the rubble. Since the news that James Gunn and his producing partner Peter Safran have been ousted as co-CEOs and cochairmen of DC Studios, there are many questions about the future of their DC Universe. Chief among them is Lanterns. So is Lanterns canceled? That’s what everyone wants to know.

The confusion started when Variety published its report about the exit of Gunn and Safran on November 10. The brunt of the story details how Gunn and Safran’s leadership of the studio was a modest, but not big enough, success for Warner Bros. (and now Skydance) brass. “Efforts to right the ship were mixed at best—with tentpoles and television shows that didn’t always generate enough excitement to justify their price tags,” wrote Variety. “Sources suggest the thinking was they spent big—and lost too much—on projects that just weren’t resonating with audiences.”

In the story, Variety explored the studio’s TV efforts, which we should note have plenty of asterisks. Under Gunn and Safran’s leadership, DC Studios released The Penguin, a spin-off of The Batman that won awards; the caveat is that it was already in development before Gunn and Safran took over. There was also the second season of Peacemaker, a series created by Gunn (as a spin-off of his 2021 movie The Suicide Squad) but whose creation also, weirdly, predates Gunn’s reign over DC. It was also the second season of a television show that premiered three years earlier.

This leads to Lanterns, the first DC show to grow from conception to air under Gunn and Safran. In Variety’s original version of its story, the outlet’s phrasing about the fate of Lanterns made the Internet jump, as it suggested the show, which cultivated a dedicated audience late in the summer, was canceled. “An insider at the company suggests the crime drama isn’t quite capturing the zeitgeist, pointing out the show didn’t get renewed for a second season,” Variety wrote.

Hours later, Variety changed its story to its current phrasing (the new words in bold): “Yet an insider at the company suggests the crime drama isn’t quite capturing the zeitgeist, pointing out the show has yet to be renewed for a second season.”

Variety also added the same source, saying, “People cannot wait to make another season of a hit show.”

You can see why there’s confusion. Gunn and Safran’s dismissal from DC was sudden, blindsiding even the men themselves (according to trades like The Hollywood Reporter) at a time when DC Studios seemed to be in a steady rhythm. Sure, this year’s Supergirl was a fumble, but last year’s Superman had gotten the refreshed DCU off on the right foot, and the recent mid-budget horror thriller Clayface grossed a clean $100 million. Though none of the Gunn/Safran–era movies were eye-watering successes, you also can’t measure long-term impact and sustained popularity of a franchise in box-office milestones alone. That’s not how art works.

It’s especially confusing with Lanterns. Back in October, outlets like Variety reported strong viewership figures for the series on HBO and HBO Max. In Skydance’s own third-quarter earnings call, the studio touted Lanterns as a rousing success, with an average of 22 million global viewers, “among HBO Max’s strongest launches” ever for the platform. To now read from insider sources claiming Lanterns wasn’t “quite capturing the zeitgeist” is downright absurd and frankly an insult to anyone’s intelligence.

Is Lanterns canceled? The good news is it isn’t. The bad news is that it isn’t canceled yet. Lanterns was a key part of Gunn’s grand plans for the DCU. Now that he’s been removed—and his future at the company unclear, despite more projects in development under his guidance—the fate of Lanterns is uncertain, as is the whole DCU.

A likely scenario is that Skydance will burn through all the remaining DC projects and dump them on HBO Max—stuff like Gunn’s Superman sequel Man of Tomorrow, the mockumentary comedy The People vs. Gorilla Grodd, and the animated film Dynamic Duo, which is said to be a highly experimental project using puppetry and animation. Lanterns season 2 could be among them, perhaps fast-tracked to HBO Max, where it would get a swift cancellation regardless of its performance (especially now that audiences will know not to get invested), or it simply doesn’t get made at all. But it’s anyone’s guess what will actually happen.

Whether audiences will get to see John Stewart be the Green Lantern is now in the hands of new studio leaders like Jeremy Latcham, who will replace Gunn and Safran starting December 1. We’ll see if Latcham leads with willpower or through fear.
