#6821. Tommy Lee Jones Couldn’t Care Less About His Legacy

The screen legend has more interesting things going on—including a stellar turn on The Lowdown season 2, as an estranged, crusty old father.

Tommy Lee Jones, 80, an Oscar winner and a star of the second season of The Lowdown, the noirish Tulsa crime story undercut with terrific dime-store waggery, sits flat-footed in the green room at Good Morning America, in downtown Manhattan. He has just done an on-camera interview with Ethan Hawke, who stars as the show’s protagonist. In the next dressing room are many adorable, pawing puppies being primped for a segment on National Adopt-a-Dog Month. Jones is adorable too, in his avuncular way, his face an old-dog scowl that softens when you ask the right question, as if you’d scratched him in the right spot.

On The Lowdown, he plays Hawke’s estranged father, a crusty old bastard who holds secrets. There’s a bad history between them, and Hawke’s character, an impulsive, truth-seeking investigative journalist, at first wants nothing to do with his dad, but that situation evolves. They’re both named Lee Raybon.

Jones wears a glen-plaid sport coat, a blue dress shirt, and comfortable sneakers. When the interview begins, he looks at you, ready but giving nothing until he’s asked to.

ESQUIRE: I just watched the first six episodes of this season, and man, I wasn’t ready for [REDACTED].

TOMMY LEE JONES: Oh, you watched all those episodes? The [REDACTED] scene was—I hope that was kind of shocking.

We didn’t see it coming, which I guess is a testament to you all.

That’s the whole point. You didn’t see it coming. That’s the reason we did it.

The way you developed your character’s way of speaking, the way he trails off here and there, tells us that this guy knows some stuff he’s not giving away. He’s shifty. It’s just brilliant. I’m just trying to give a compliment, not really asking a question.

You said to Charlie Rose in an interview a long time ago that there’s no through line that runs through the characters you play. At the same time, you don’t take just any role that comes along. Do you ever think about legacy? How are they going to remember Tommy Lee Jones? Or is it really just “This role would be a challenge to play, so I’m going to take it?”

I don’t have long-term thinking, and I don’t even think in a term like legacy. I think more like a dog. I’m absorbed with what’s in front of me. I might run off and chase a squirrel.

I believe you. It’s just that I’ve started thinking recently, What am I writing about? Is there a body of work here? I hadn’t thought that way before. With each story it was just This is a cool story. I’ll do this story.

That’s what I thought. I thought this show was really cool. The people that were working on it were cool, and Tulsa is an extremely cool place. Ethan made a good point on TV just now when he said good noir stories always have a sense of place, so the location was very important. It’s true about film noir, and this show really qualifies as part of that genre. A keen sense of place, leading to inarguable originality.

How far are Tulsa and eastern Oklahoma from where you live in Texas?

Oh, I don’t know. It’s northeast of Dallas. So I think the drive up—

[He turns to his wife of of 25 years, Dawn, who sits in the corner looking at her phone.]

How long would it take to drive to Tulsa from San Antonio?

When I was in prep school, we went up to Oklahoma to play a school in football, and they always cheated. Also when I was in high school, they would sell 3.2 beer to minors, and it was fun, a big adventure every once in a while, to pile into a car and drive to Oklahoma to buy a beer.

Ethan’s hilarious on The Lowdown, but you’re also very funny! Always have been. The warden in Natural Born Killers, Dwight McClusky, is your funniest role, I feel. Samuel Gerard in The Fugitive. And then I’m watching this show and was reminded how funny you can be. How would you describe your sense of humor? What’s funny to you?

People. People are hilarious. The best humor is found in human behavior. Any student of human behavior is going to be highly amused, when they’re not horrified.

Did you watch stuff as a kid that made you laugh?

The Three Stooges. Slapstick. Warner Brothers cartoons. That’s fundamental humor. I mean, if I see a fat lady slip on a banana peel, I swear I’m going to laugh.

You share some beautiful scenes with the young actress who plays your granddaughter, Ryan Kiera Armstrong. I couldn’t think of many films in which we see you spend a lot of time with children.

There was a movie called River Rats, and I was on a boat for a good part of this movie with two children, one of whom was Martha Plimpton, as a little slip of a girl, playing my daughter. We became good friends on that, and I was happy to see her on The Lowdown.

You and Ethan, and Martha now too, are seasoned veterans. Is the approach different when you’re working with young Ryan, who doesn’t have 50 films under her belt? Is there a greater need with a child actor to get to know each other before filming?

Oh, actors are actors, so it’s not difficult for me to relate to an actor regardless of their age. And she’s a pro. Actors, they have a way of getting to know one another very quickly.

Yeah, she is. And she’s a fearless and admirable young lady.

[Shit.] Not from you. I don’t mean in your scenes. I mean her other scenes.

Getting back to the idea of a through line: If I may point out one commonality across your performances, it’s that your characters have wisdom. You yourself are obviously well-read. You went to Harvard, and, for example, you’ve compared the satire of Natural Born Killers to Molière. Is reading still a big part of your life?

I’ve been making a study of Texas history, which begins with—well, there is no beginning. It’s always been there. But its experience as an American territory, for me, begins with James K. Polk. I read a political biography of Polk. That’s a good place to start a study of modern Texas history.

Yeah, well, he was Andrew Jackson’s bitch.

Getting back to Lee Raybon Sr., whose nickname is Frog, and his son. Fathers and sons are always rich territory.

Ethan’s character changes his attitude toward you. It gets better. Did you see that as Frog’s project, what this character is after—his son’s love?

[Mildly annoyed.] Well, I compared myself to a dog earlier because I really deal with what’s in front of me.

And all the qualities that you see in that character are ones that Sterlin Harjo, the show’s creator, wrote. We learn that this character is dying. That slowly begins to dawn on us, and we see that he’s trying to somehow reconcile life with his son and get to know his granddaughter. But that quest in this character is not advertised. It’s discovered as we watch things unfold in an often amusing way, and always original. That was appealing. I mean, I think that’s the way it works.

Can I, at the risk of pissing you off: I know you say that you’re like a dog. Are you telling me that—

I don’t want to belabor that too much. I’m a little bit like a dog. [Laughs, kind of.]

But this is what I’m getting at. I’ve seen every film you’ve ever made, and you don’t give a lot of interviews, but in the ones I’ve seen over the years you’ve made it clear that each character is its own separate project. No connections. But at the same time, I know how smart you are, and so part of me thinks, Come on. Of course you must see some continuum along the characters you play. We see similarities among characters in literature, even across different authors in different centuries. The through line of all your parts is that you chose them. So pushing back a tiny bit, I’m suggesting that your performance as Lee Raybon has inherited something from these previous men that you’ve inhabited in the last 50 years. Is that fair at all?

[Breathing a sigh of relief, and perhaps satisfaction.] Okay.

[Pauses, hoping this line of questioning was over.] Oh. Well, I don’t know. I’m not aware of any continuum. [Any sense of Esquire’s relief or satisfaction disappears.] I don’t think it’s in my best interest, and I’m not inclined to think about what all the characters I play have in common. The only thing they have in common is that I’m playing them. And they’re all built out of experience. Let me refer you to an essay by T.S. Eliot called “Tradition and the Individual Talent.” It’s an indication of what happens to actors, really. Their performances are made out of the shards and the pieces and the bits of your experience of life and your thinking about it. One of the reasons you read that essay is so you have a better understanding of The Waste Land or even James Joyce. Modern art is comprised of the shards of experience, reassembled in a way that has a dramatic form, with a beginning, a middle, an end, and a purpose.

Do you see a performance such as this one as a piece of art?

No. Not really. I don’t know what it—you know, this is the thing I never give any thought to: What is art?

Compare your view of God when you were six or seven years old to now.

[Coming to life a bit.] Oh! Well? I never imagined a man in a white robe with a long white beard sitting on a throne mounted on a cloud with some lightning bolts in his hand. That’s one image. It’s probably almost as good as any other, but the question is, you either believe in such a thing as God or you don’t. I do.

Has your view or understanding changed over your life?

Not really. My grandmother and my aunts and my mother always took me to a Southern Baptist church. I was baptized in the Southern Baptist church. And, like all organized religions, there’s a good deal of nonsense in it. But it also leads to an awareness of the deity, which by definition exceeds our capability to define it.

[A long pause as Jones stares at the floor, then looks up, eyebrows raised and smiling.]

That was pretty good. I’m gonna write that down.

If you had to cook a meal for dinner, what’s the best thing you could come up with?

[For the first time during the interview, Jones’s body relaxes—arm over the back of the chair.]

I’d probably buy some pork ribs, an entire side of a little young pig, and slowly barbecue that over a mesquite fire and serve it with a mixture of sautéed yellow squash and onions and probably a sweet potato. You can scratch them with a fork and rub them down with butter, wrap them up in tinfoil and cook them alongside the pig right over the fire. You can feed a lot of people that way, and it tastes good. And it’s fun to eat.

There’s a product you can buy in San Saba, Texas, called Bill’s Season All. Bill’s Season All is something you sprinkle on pork ribs and it makes them taste real good. I don’t know what all’s in it, but it’s delectable.

Texas chili: Do you have strong opinions?

[Jones snaps to even more, speaking a little louder and with some of the gusto and urgency of U.S. Marshal Samuel Gerard saying, “Listen up, ladies and gentlemen. Our fugitive has been on the run for 90 minutes. Average foot speed over uneven ground barring injuries is four miles an hour, and that gives us a radius of six miles. What I want out of each and every one of you is a hard-target search of every gas station, residence, warehouse, farmhouse, henhouse, outhouse, and doghouse in that area. Checkpoints go up at 15 miles. Your fugitive’s name is Dr. Richard Kimble. Go get him.”]

Don’t use ground beef. You need to cube it. Cube that beef into pieces about the size of the dice that you play Monopoly with. And after that, your own creativity comes in with the spices. But it’s important to avoid ground beef. And you don’t put beans in chili. That’s on the side. That’s at the discretion of the diner. Some people just like chili, but they just don’t necessarily want to see a bunch of beans floating in it. You’ll find one recipe in the Hill Country of Texas and another one in the hills above Santa Fe. Quite different.

Last night, I was watching either the fifth or sixth episode of The Lowdown, and Martha Plimpton’s character talks about putting a couple spoonfuls of Tang in chili.

I can’t imagine putting Tang in chili. But chili is like gumbo. Or Kentucky burgoo. Or Irish stew. It’s a food that’s in and of the people, so the way of cooking it and the ingredients vary from place to place and from circumstance to circumstance. Real gumbo has always got—well, it can have anything in it. Duck … goose … whatever crawled across the porch last night. Chili’s the same kind of thing. It’s the food of the people, and as such, the main ingredients are whatever you’ve got.

I don’t think so. I’m closer to profligate than frugal. We spend a lot of money on horses. My wife is one of the better female polo players in the United States. I played polo for 40-some-odd years. I’m a bit too old for it now. “Old and worn-out” is what they would call it.

Where did you live when you lived here in New York?

Yeah, it was. When I first came here in 1969, the streets were covered in dog shit. And it really got to be bad. Land mines. They passed some ordinances in the city that people had to clean up after their dogs. You’d have to be very careful walking down the street. Nothing more embarrassing than going into a meeting with dog shit on your shoes.

So now that you’ve turned in this astonishingly beautiful performance as Lee Raybon—

—and are there hills left to conquer as an actor? Are there types of roles you have yet to play?

I don’t know. There’s a schedule for the day. And I looked at it, but I don’t really—I’d have to ask her. That’s the reason I don’t have to know. I have her.

Okay, well, I appreciate this. I hope it wasn’t too painful and I didn’t—

I know talking about acting is not the most interesting thing.

Well, it’s breakfast time. When it’s first thing in the morning at home, if your cook’s not there and you’re hungry, what could you whip up?

I have a French press for coffee, a toaster for a bagel. And we’ve always got cream cheese and smoked salmon. Bagel, lox, cream cheese, coffee, and a grapefruit. That’s what I ordinarily eat for breakfast.

You’re right, this is more interesting than talking about acting.

Oh, breakfast. Breakfast is more interesting than anything.

#6817. How Olivia Munn Takes the Edge Off

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The actress shares the beauty and self-care essentials she swears by, including a $75 volumizing cream

Welcome to Take the Edge Off, a series that breaks down the beauty and self-care routines of influencers, CEOs, experts, and celebrities. Find out how they unwind and decompress, and take a closer look at the holy-grail essentials they’re raving about.

Life can throw unexpected surprises—good and bad—your way, and many lean on wellness practices to stay grounded in chaos. Olivia Munn knows this firsthand.

The actress was diagnosed with early-stage Luminal B breast cancer in early 2023, thanks to what she refers to as the “life-saving” risk assessment test with renowned obstetric and gynecologic surgeon Thaïs Aliabadi at the helm. With a toddler and fresh off a double mastectomy, Munn turned to meditation to find her footing through it all.

“I always associated meditation with having to quiet your mind, and I couldn’t imagine how anyone actually did that, but over time, I’ve come to understand that it isn’t about stopping the noise in your head; it’s about learning not to be carried away by it,” Munn tells Bazaar. “I don’t feel the need to chase every thought or immediately make sense of everything. I can just let things settle without feeling responsible for figuring them all out.”

That same strength and balance are exactly what fashion label Anne Klein wanted to embody when the brand enlisted Munn as ambassador for its fall/winter 2026 collection and to continue spreading her message.

Ahead, we caught up with Munn about what else keeps her present during times of stress, from the manifestation candle she loves to light to words she lives by.

“The future you is depending on the you today to do the right thing.” This means the me today depended on the me three years ago to go to the doctor, get the MRI, and face cancer head-on. I was depending on the me last week, month, or year to start working out more, learn that new language, or organize my closet. When I think about how fast time goes by, and how tomorrow, next month, and next year come in the blink of an eye, it motivates me to take the first step toward whatever I want to do.

A couple of months ago, I began using prescription-strength azelaic acid and tretinoin. I start with azelaic acid, then tretinoin, and finish with the Volume Cream by Adipeau, my holy-grail moisturizer.

My favorite cream right now is Adipeau’s Volume Cream. I’ve reached the point in my life where my hands and neck are showing signs of being crepey. A friend told me about this cream and promised me that if I used it every day for a month, I’d see a difference, and she was right. The science behind it is incredible, so incredible that I often worry about whether the company is doing well because I need them to stay in business forever.

My makeup goal has always been no-makeup makeup. But it is incredibly difficult to master, especially on yourself. My hat’s off to all the influencers who do it so beautifully; I see you and wish I were you.

Shige brand cuticle oil. The amazing manicurist Shige [Taylor] created her own nail line, beginning with an incredible cuticle oil made in Japan. [My] nail bed has never looked or felt so hydrated.

Yes, I light candles from Aloha Elixir. They’re made in Hawaii and are really special, intentional candles.

When I start getting anxiety, which I often do, or have difficulty falling asleep, I do this breathing technique that activates the parasympathetic nervous system, which controls our “fight-or-flight” response: Breathe in through your nose for four beats, hold for seven, breathe out through your nose for eight beats. It calms my system down, and the anxiety goes away, or I drift off to sleep. I do it three to six times, sometimes a little more. But it always works for me.

#6815. I Paid a Matchmaker $250,000 to Find My Soulmate. The Result Surprised Me.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Sure, I could go on Tinder, swipe through profiles, and find women interested in me. But that’s a long process, and I don’t want to put in the same effort I did 20 or 30 years ago.

Dating apps have become a special kind of hell for singles. People are sick of swiping. They’re tired of the road-to-nowhere conversations and disappointing dates. For all their predictive power, algorithms simply aren’t very good at finding the one.

As a result, people are dumping Tinder left and right. But they haven’t given up on love entirely. In fact, quite the opposite. After years of letting the apps play Cupid, they’re calling in the experts. Google searches for “matchmaker” more than doubled between January 2025 and January 2026. And as interest in matchmaking grows, prices are climbing too. Average fees jumped 26 percent last year. Prices vary enormously, but at the highest end of the market, finding a girlfriend can cost more than a Ferrari.

For the latest installment of “The Secret Lives of Men,” we spoke to Henry*, a 70-year-old man from Atlanta who spent $250,000 on a matchmaker. His fee covered a year of personalized matchmaking, complete with a wardrobe consultant, dating coaches, personality tests, and a nationwide search for eligible women. After eight months and roughly ten introductions, Henry finally met someone he liked.

*Name and identifying details have been changed to protect anonymity.

I’m what I would call a silver fox. I’m 70 years old now, and I’m in excellent physical, mental, and financial shape. I’ve been married twice and dated plenty of women. Over the years, I’ve tried just about every way to meet someone: bars, introductions through friends, dating sites, apps. But at this stage of my life, I can afford the very best—so I decided to shell out $250,000 for a top-of-the-line matchmaker.

Some people spend $250,000 on a car or $75,000 on a vacation in Positano. Others fly private. Sure, you could spend a fraction of that money, but you’d get a lesser experience. I look at matchmaking the same way. Sure, I could go on Tinder, swipe through profiles, and find women interested in me. But that’s a long process, and I don’t want to put in the same effort I did 20 or 30 years ago. I’m interested in finding someone I’m actually compatible with.

I’ve actually used cheaper matchmakers before, the kind who charge around $25,000 for a few months. Mostly they just save you the trouble of going through dating apps yourself. They’ll find a few women on Tinder, a few more somewhere else, and send you their profiles. This time I wanted someone who would actually get to know me and put some thought into whom she set me up with. That’s why I hired Sameera Sullivan. She works with only three or four clients at a time and charges $250,000 for a year of services that go far beyond what the other matchmakers offered. Those guys were like a Lexus. Sameera is a Bugatti.

The first thing she did was bring in a few professionals to help with my dating profile. A wardrobe consultant went through my clothes and suggested a few changes. A photographer took my pictures, and a professional writer put together my bio. Then I had several sessions with a dating coach and took a bunch of personality tests to figure out what kind of woman I’d be most compatible with.

Each date I went on helped Sameera narrow things down further. After every introduction, she’d ask both me and the woman she’d set me up with how it went. Even if we stopped seeing each other after two or three dates, she’d try to figure out why and use what she learned to make a better match next time. It’s kind of like AI. The more information she had, the better the results. The tenth woman she introduced me to ended up being the one. Sameera actually found her on LinkedIn and reached out to see if she’d be interested in going out with me.

We’ve been dating for almost three months now, and things are going extremely well. Everyone says we have big couples energy. She’s in her mid-50s, which is good because I look and act younger than my biological age. Our love languages match, our personalities complement each other, and I feel more compatible with her than I did with either of my former wives.

One thing I really like about her is that she’s got a bit of an adventurous streak. I’m naturally pretty steady and set in my ways, but I have an adventurous side too, and she brings it out in me. Like the other night, we were having dinner at a Mexican restaurant, and someone mentioned there was a speakeasy downstairs. Normally I would’ve said we should try it another time. But I saw her face light up, so I said, “Okay, let’s go.” We went in and had a drink. I probably wouldn’t have done that on my own.

I’ve put my matchmaking contract on pause for now. If this relationship doesn’t work out, I still have about four months of service left. For now, I’m very happy with how things are going. The funniest thing about it all, though, is that the woman Sameera found for me, the one she searched all over the country for—she actually lives in my neighborhood.

#6814. ‘We Just Are So Gagged’: Jordan Firstman and Reggie Absolom on Club Kid’s Success

The film’s stars discuss their excitement, their music tastes, and how they interpret that ending.

This story contains spoilers for Club Kid.

For his debut feature film, Club Kid, Jordan Firstman envisioned a curly-haired boy who could live in a world of adults. The story was well established by the time casting agent Carmen Cuba met British actor Reggie Absolom, who had appeared in Silo and other series. Firstman, who wrote, directed, and starred in the film, knew he was the perfect one to play Arlo, the 10-year-old son that Firstman’s character Peter never knew he had. Firstman and Absolom grew close quickly and developed a unique friendship that hasn’t slowed down post-filming. “We text all day,” Firstman says.

The pair spoke to ELLE about Reggie getting to DJ for the film, whether he’ll have a Radiohead phase, and what the movie says about family.

Jordan Firstman: I had a very extensive list of things I wanted in the character, and my casting director was like, “You really need to manage your expectations because you’re looking for a child actor. Baseline, you have to find someone good.” I also just wanted them to be naturally cool and not feel like a child actor [but] feel like a kid. I wanted them to have curly hair. I wanted them to look enough like me that they could be my kid. In the script, the character is written as half Indian, and she was like, “Babe, you’re just not going to find all of that.”

I found Reggie, and he checked every box. He looked like the kid I had in my head when I wrote it. He also was a really natural actor and nothing felt forced. I sent him a playlist of songs I wrote the movie to, and he told me which ones he liked. I thought he had really good taste, and we just talked for a while and got ice cream. And then it wasn’t until in the process that I found out he was a quarter Indian too. He literally had every single thing that I wanted, and so it did feel like a miracle in a lot of ways.

Reggie Absolom: It was a bit weird at first. But then I became such good friends with everyone anyway and it just felt like I fit in there. It didn’t matter how old I was. I was just friends with everyone, and I was just working like they were as well.

JF: I love sending people that I love music. Reggie’s music taste influenced the characters. He was into Slipknot at the time, so I wrote a little Slipknot into the script, and he also liked Massive Attack and Cocteau Twins, once I sent him my playlist. I wrote that in.

There’s this whole other component of the actual club music, on which we collaborated so closely with David Peck, who’s in the movie [and who wrote songs for the film and performed them as Heather Asylum]. He did Reggie’s whole DJ set and River Moon consulted. We had real DJs in the scene helping me and making sure we had all the right tracks. The music in the movie is everything; it’s the throughline in the story and the construction of the movie.

RA: You get most of the credit. You gave me quite a few songs, like Cocteau Twins ones and really good Massive Attack ones. I was like, okay, I’m going to go deeper into these and listen to all of their albums. Now there’s some songs that I would’ve never really listened to. When I listened to Cocteau Twins, I would’ve been like, “Okay, this is weird.” But now I’m like, “Okay, maybe I like the weird kind of songs.”

JF: I think you’re going to also have a major Radiohead era.

RA: Maybe, but I don’t know. I don’t like Radiohead.

JF: We were going to do “Maybe I’m Amazed” by Paul McCartney, and he wasn’t responding to our emails about licensing it. The day before [we filmed the scene], I was trying to think of other songs, and “Thoroughfare” is my favorite song, I think ever. I feel like I would be that dad that made my kid play Ethel Cain on the piano with me. Then we played it, and it felt really cute and really sincere. We still sang Paul McCartney, but they were like, “You don’t need Paul McCartney. Don’t waste your money.” And “Thoroughfare” is the vibe.

RA: It got me into a lot of different types of music, which I’m happy about. DJing was really fun and something that I’d want to do in the future. Maybe not a full-time job, but something on the side to do, something fun.

RA: It didn’t really influence the roles I wanted to get, but now that I’m in a film that’s done so well, it’s opened up for me to maybe get bigger roles than I would’ve expected before filming this.

RA: The idea of being in a movie, that was the next step for me. I was filming maybe three episodes of a series. I felt like no matter the role, just being in the movie would be the next thing to do. Then the fact that this was a lead role and that I felt similar to Arlo personality-wise in some ways. I felt like I could perform really naturally with the things that Arlo says and the way that he acts.

JF: Kramer vs. Kramer is such a classic and a movie I love. I often reference An Unmarried Woman with Jill Clayburgh. I love her relationship with her daughter in that movie and how they treat each other like adults. Because Peter has no experience with kids, he doesn’t know how to treat them like anything else but how he treats everyone.

JF: Once I started writing it, I started really understanding Peter and Arlo and what they needed. I actually feel like it’s a gift that they didn’t end up together for both of their lives for that time. That phone call [at the end] is so important, because the door opens again once they’re ready. I think it would’ve been too intense for Peter to [raise Arlo full-time]....He still wasn’t completely out of the party scene. He was still selling drugs. He was not ready to really do this in a real way. Arlo really needed a year of knowing true stability and a normal childhood.

But I think what Peter and his community offered Arlo was a very different version of normal. They encouraged creativity. For a traumatized kid, having an art practice is everything. In my darkest times as a kid, I turned to art. To me, it’s a very human ending, and to me it’s hopeful.

RA: Both of them needed to have that break. They’re trying to find themselves and what they should do. Now, another thing, whilst Arlo was with Peter, he was getting no education really. He tried to get him into school.

RA: He just gave up on that. The ending also leaves it up to interpretation what happens to them in the future. Obviously, it could have been like, oh, they rejoin and they’re father and son forever. I think it’s nicer when the film has an ending that’s up to interpretation a bit, where there could be different branches on the ending.

JF: He really enjoyed fatherhood, so I think he will continue his relationship with Arlo and help him through his teen years and grow up.

RA: In my opinion, Peter with a baby, adopting a baby would be a bit, like Edison says, “He’s all prepared. Forget the trauma,” or something like that.

RA: He could do it. He’d need Oscar [Peter’s partner, played by Diego Calva].

JF: No one involved in this project has had this happen to them. Even Cara [Delevingne] has never been a part of something like this. We just are so gagged. We can’t believe this is happening and that people are responding to it in this way. We get standing ovations everywhere we go, and it’s surreal. We don’t take for granted that this is a rare thing that happens.

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.

#6812. Dialed In: All the Time in the World

Accutron’s bold new Horizon Chronograph unites all the best elevated complications in watchmaking in a single watch at a fantastic price.

Every now and then, a watch comes along that demolishes all your preconceived notions of the way things are in watchmaking. This week, Accutron released the Horizon, an entirely new timepiece (in two colorways, blue and black) that positively bristles with complications normally associated with five- and six-figure watches and at a very attractive price to boot.

It's a bold move from the brand that created the very first fully electronic watch back in 1960. With that model, Accutron debuted its tuning fork technology, by which a traditional mechanical watch escapement, with all its susceptibility to friction and gravity, was replaced with a tiny metal tuning fork powered by electricity via a built-in transistor. What made the tuning fork ground-breaking was the fact that, under power, it vibrated at a constant and continuous rate. And it made the aptly named Accutron (hence the name) accurate to plus or minus one second a month, an unimagined degree of accuracy at that point, and, frankly, for a long time after too.

Back to the Horizon. First up in a litany of nifty and rather mind-blowing gizmos is the fact that this Master Complication tells you the time not only with the hands of the watch in the conventional way, but also with sound. This watch is a minute repeater, chiming the current time in hours, then quarter hours, then minutes at the press of a button. Derived in the 18th century from the need to know the time at night a good century before electric light and SuperLuminova markers made their appearance, the minute repeater could—once you got the hang of it—tell you the exact time even in pitch darkness. But it's a complication so rare and expensive to make, that we honestly can't think of a minute repeater ever produced at such a compelling price.

The bells and whistles don't stop there. With three sub dials in glittery black sandstone that looks like the night sky) set into a granular dial made from Denchu, another granular sandstone, all beneath the conventional center hands, this watch is both a Perpetual Calendar and Chronograph. It features the day and the date, the leap year, the phases of the moon, a second time zone and a day/night indicator, all packed into an elegant and sporty 41.5mm stainless steel case. This, by the way, is made in a superior 904L steel with a matching steel bracelet, an alloy known for its deep luster when polished, as well as its resistance to corrosion and pitting. Underlying all this style is the Horizon's substance; the watch is driven by a quartz movement that offers excellent accuracy.

If you want to understand the meaning of time, you only have to gaze into the Horizon's dial, which breaks it all down for you in a very cool package. Reading it will keep you busy for days.

#6809. Glee Is Getting a Reboot Series 11 Years Later: Everything We Know So Far

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Glee has always held a special place in Ryan Murphy’s heart, so when he heard the show was having a major resurgence on social media, it planted the seeds for a reboot in his mind.

“That show is interesting because it’s come back around,” he said in a July interview with People. “So many young people now watch it. And I’m like, ‘Maybe we should revisit that show.’ My nostalgia and love for that show is enough that I’m like, ‘Well, maybe people would like to see a new incarnation of that in some capacity.’ So yeah, I love that show, and I loved what it became and what it meant to so many people.”

Months later, Murphy delivered on his comments—on October 21, Disney+ and Hulu granted a series order for Glee, 17 years after the original show’s premiere and 11 years after the final episode. The series also announced the news on its official Instagram, writing, “It’s official. Glee is returning.”

Details about the reboot are currently sparse, but here’s everything we know so far.

Yes. In September, Murphy told Entertainment Tonight that there has been progress with writing the show. “We have a script. Brad [Falchuk], Ian [Brennan], and I, who wrote all the first two seasons, have written something,” he said.

The original cast of Glee included Lea Michele, Kevin McHale, Chris Colfer, Darren Criss, Jane Lynch, Amber Riley, Jenna Ushkowitz, Dianna Agron, Heather Morris, Matthew Morrison, Mark Salling, Jonathan Groff, Chord Overstreet, and Harry Shum Jr. Cory Monteith and Naya Rivera were also part of the original cast, but died in 2013 and 2020, respectively.

While Murphy did not state which stars he has been talking to, he told Entertainment Tonight that he’s “been in touch with around 10 of the original cast members. It’s very sweet, and they all had a lot of questions, but they all were very enthusiastic about the idea.”

Hours after the reboot news was revealed, Variety reported that Michele will reportedly join the cast of the reboot. “She’s in,” a source told the outlet, while a second source added, “It’s still early-ish days, but it feels good and we’re happy to get the band back together.” Murphy and Michele did not provide a comment.

Following the news, Morris hinted that she’d be stepping back into Brittany S. Pierce’s cheerleading uniform. “Yes ladies and gentle men of women, rumors are true. Brittany S Pierce was in fact lost in the sewers for all these years. But I’m here to inform you that she is alive and well…and back for more #Glee,” she captioned a throwback photo of herself from the set.

While the post feels like a confirmation, Murphy has not confirmed any of the cast members who will return for the show.

Overstreet and McHale also hinted that they’d be returning. Overstreet wrote, “reunited and fit feels so good!” after the reboot news dropped. McHale reacted to a tweet that said Drake has surpassed the Glee cast with the most Billboard Hot 100 chart entries. “Challenge accepted,” he replied to the tweet.

On October 23, Deadline offered an update on Morris’s and Overstreet’s statuses. The outlet reported that they were in talks to join the reboot.

During his November 11 appearance on The Daily Beast’s Obsessed podcast, Colfer revealed that he’s planning to have more “conversations” about potentially joining the reboot.

He added, “It was really hard going back to the show after Cory passed, and I’m sure it’d be really difficult, especially now that Naya’s gone too,” Colfer said. “But I guess you have to think, are you going to let the loss of two people you love very much prevent you from being around a dozen other people you love as well? There’s a lot to think about with it.”

Colfer does have ideas on what his character, Kurt Hummel, would be up to in the current timeline, though. “I would love to see him like a Miranda Priestly-type, running his own fashion blog or a magazine,” he shared. “I could also see him on tour with Chappell Roan, styling her. There’s a lot of fun avenues they could explore.”

Over the years, several cast members have shared their reactions to a potential Glee reboot, with some stating they would not return to the series if it were made.

Michele, who played the show’s central main character, Rachel Berry, told E! News in September that she’d be open to following Murphy if he expanded the Glee universe. “I love Glee, I will always love Glee,” she said. “I love Ryan Murphy; it holds such a special place in my heart for me, so I will follow Ryan wherever he goes.”

Colfer told Bustle in an interview earlier this month that Glee “was a really tough show to do, and it would be really hard to do it now that Naya [Rivera] is gone.” He added, “I do know that Glee fans would probably come after me with pitchforks and jazz hands if I said no.”

Lynch reflected on her Glee character, Sue Sylvester, in an interview with Entertainment Tonight earlier this month and confirmed that she would join the cast again in a heartbeat. “I would be in like that,” she said.

In May 2025, Riley, who starred as Mercedes Jones, said that she wouldn’t be interested in returning to Glee during her appearance on Monét X Change’s Monét Talks podcast.

“I just feel like what we did was so phenomenal, I don’t want to risk trying to reinvent the wheel,” she said. “Now if they redo it, I think that’s amazing, because there are so many kids now that are just now discovering Glee, and so I think that that’d be really dope. But, you know, we lost cast members. I just feel like the chemistry, that formula, was so specific, and it’s changed now, so I don't know if it would be as effective. I’m always prepared for that question.”

Morris (Brittany S. Pierce) shared in a 2024 interview that she would be down to join the reboot if it were made. “I would,” she said in a video interview. “I think the film industry has come a long way. It was a really tough show to do, and I think being in the space that I’m [in] now, absolutely. I yearn so bad to be in a musical again.”

In 2023, Criss (Blaine Anderson) discussed a potential reboot and revealed that he would be on the fence about it.

“I’m not against it. I know people would like that. I think what made Glee so special is where it sat in its specific time and place,” he explained during his PeopleCon appearance. “I think a lot of cultural moments are defined by the alchemy of where we’re at as a society, and the way it called and responded to each other made it really special.”

Criss continued, “While I, as a fan of Glee, would go, ‘Yeah, I would love to see more,’ I do think there’s something we have to remember about the why, when, and how [the show was] special being very specific to one time. I wouldn’t be against it, but I’m also not screaming to do it. I’m somewhere in between. They have to make it really count.”

At AdoptTogether’s annual Los Angeles Baby Ball in 2022, Ushkowitz (Tina Cohen-Chang) told E! News that she would be open to a reboot if the right team worked together to bring it to life.

“If Ryan was doing it and the right people were involved,” she told the outlet at the time. “I would consider it.” She also dispelled rumors of drama between members of the cast, adding, “We’re a family—families don’t always have to get along. When we were working together, it took more energy to fight than it did to just get along. I spent more hours with them than literally, my own family.”

#6808. Design Experts Reveal When It’s Actually OK to Start Decorating for the Holidays

There’s no hard rule—but your HOA may have one.

Now that the Halloween candy bowls are empty, you might find thoughts of tinsel, garland, and fairy lights dancing in your head, but is it really time to trade your pumpkins for holiday trimmings? Some have strong opinions on the topic, half believing the jolly spirit of the holiday season doesn't start until after Thanksgiving, the other rushing to unbox their silver bells as soon as the clock strikes 12 on November 1—but the choice isn’t always yours to make.

If you belong to a Homeowners’ Association (and over 78.1 million Americans do), the question of when to put up your outdoor décor may be dictated by their guidelines. HOAs tend to have rules about holiday decorations, restrictions covering their location (within the boundaries of your yard, of course), sound level (not too loud), safety (no open flames), light color, and when the decorations can be installed. In some communities, you can even be fined for bringing out the twinkle lights and nativity creche before Thanksgiving—this happened to one Florida man in 2002, when he was hit with a fine of $1,000 for putting his lights up on November 6.

If you don’t live in an HOA, you may not get fined for decorating too early, but you will get judged by neighbors who find pre-Thanksgiving holiday displays a little, well, tacky. To be fair, holding until after Thanksgiving does have its perks—why would you pass on the opportunity to decorate a gorgeous fall tablescape? In the spirit of peace on earth and goodwill toward man, we asked design experts what to do. Here’s what they said.

Additional copy by Meghan Shouse and Alyssa Khan.

You might follow a religious tradition that guides your holiday decorating, like placing the Baby Jesus in the nativity on Christmas Day or keeping the tree up until Epiphany. But when it comes to secular etiquette, there are no hard-and-fast rules about when to start (or stop) decking the halls.

“Most people find it tasteful to stick within the weeks or the month of the holiday itself,” points out Lizzie Post, co-president of The Emily Post Institute and author of Emily Post’s Etiquette—The Centennial Edition. “That said, there is no etiquette that dictates it. It’s the same way you can paint your house neon orange with blue stripes—there’s nothing that says you can’t. It’s really, do you want to be the person who does?”

For designer Tan France, the answer to that question is decidedly yes. For the past decade, he and his husband have been decorating their tree on Halloween and taking it down on Valentine’s Day. “I’m British, and we don’t really do Halloween,” he explains. “When I moved here 16 years ago, I was more obsessed with Christmas. So on Halloween day, when everyone else is crafting their costumes, I want to be decorating the tree. And a tree brings my husband and me a lot of joy when we come down and turn the lights on first thing in the morning. It’s just a lovely thing to perk your day up.”

Do people question France about his decision to put up decorations so early, and for so long? “Oh gosh, yeah, everyone asks that,” he says. “Online, the press, neighbors. And my answer is really simple: Why delay joy?”

If you are jonesing to do some holiday decorating, there's good news: While Christmas might be weeks away, there is one happening right now. It’s called Thanksgiving.

“Anytime before midnight of Thanksgiving is too early to decorate,” insists Wendy Kerrigan, founder of Atelier Design in New Orleans. “Filling the house with Christmas decorations too soon really takes away from the beauty of Thanksgiving, moving your mind into the bustle of Christmas.”

Kerrigan wishes more people would celebrate the fall season for longer; rushing to the tinsel takes away from the beauty of the autumn season, “Thanksgiving can be experienced in a special way too, filling the house with fall colors, fall leaves, gourds, and cornucopia,” says Kerrigan. “Instead of trying to hurry the year along, enjoy each family celebration for what it is.”

Even more, sprinting to the Yuletide season robs the holiday of its own magic. “What makes things special when they’re once a year is that they’re experiences you look forward to,” says Vermont-based interior designer Joshua Smith. “How many holidays can we take at one time? Are we doing Black Friday in July now? Let’s just enjoy the season that we’re in!”

When it comes to holiday decorating, many hands make light work. Several designers say they take advantage of the long holiday weekend and enforced family time to rope in some extra help making spirits bright. “In my parents' home, we start decorating right after Thanksgiving dinner. It’s a rare opportunity for everyone to be together, and my siblings and I stay to help out our parents,” says New York-based designer Mikel Welch. “Plus, it’s a much better tradition than terrorizing sales associates on Black Friday Eve!"

Smith has a plan for bringing the Christmas spirit to his home, without shouldering the burden of doing all the hall-decking himself. Last year, his family decorated their trees over Thanksgiving weekend and made it a tree-decorating contest. With his husband's family in town, “they’ll be divided into three teams, decorating three trees, and I’ll go around at the end judging, and everyone will win prizes. We’re not all going to be together at Christmas, so this activity is a fun way to celebrate the season and not do all the work myself,” he says.

Still, early decorators would argue that the amount of effort needed to set up holiday decor is another reason to put up decorations early—and keep them up late. “This is the most miserable time of the year; it gets dark so early, it’s so darn cold. I just think you should do everything you can to bring yourself joy,” says France. “Why put in all that effort for a few weeks?”

Even those who are normally on the side of waiting to decorate agree that desperate times call for dopamine decorating. Whether it’s the state of your inbox or the state of the world that is stressing you out, if you’re feeling a little blue, go right ahead and pull out the red and green.

“In a perfect calendar, the day after Thanksgiving would be my official start to the holiday season—it's traditionally the day when my family goes out into the woods of Tahoe National Forest to chop down our tree, drag it home, and decorate it,” says designer Noz Nozawa of Noz Design. “But this year, the day after Halloween is when I let the holidays start—there's plenty to worry about, and having my nutcrackers and string lights out a few weeks early can only boost spirits.”

Whenever you decorate, it can be a way to bring fun to a not-always joyful world. “We have no control over a lot of things in our lives, but when we walk through the door of our home, we choose to focus our energy and attention on whether that is on fear or on love and joy,” says Smith. “And this holiday season, I think more than ever we have to focus on ways to bring more light to ourselves and to each other.”

When it comes to early decor, “I can handle it from my neighbor; I cannot handle it from a corporation,” says Post. That may be because we know the Christmas displays popping up in a big-box store alongside the Halloween candy are trying to sell us something, while a neighbor decorating is spreading joy, spending their own time and money to brighten the dark winter nights. For a shop, holiday decorating is mercenary. For an individual, it’s a mitzvah.

If you appreciate the joy inspired by holiday displays and do want to start setting them up early or keep them up late, consider finding a middle ground by leaning toward decor that’s more about lighting up the night, rather than marking a particular occasion. “I see a lot of people in Vermont leaving the actual lights up, not so much like blow-up Santas or things like that, but the actual lights up for the entire winter,” says Post.

If you’re a minimalist (or live in a studio apartment), you don’t need a tree to decorate. “If you don’t have a tree, I’m going to invite you to place a hurricane lamp on your coffee table with a wreath of greenery around it and light a candle in the evening,” says Smith. “Because what we know from neuroscience and neuroaesthetics is, you’ve got to engage the senses to nourish the soul!” Light a candle or turn on some twinkle lights, and you’re liable to spark joy as well.

France would argue that even his trees aren’t occasion-specific. “Here’s the thing: I’m Muslim, and my tree and the lights on our house aren’t necessarily for Christmas,” he explains. “It’s to celebrate Thanksgiving, Christmas, and the New Year. This is the most miserable time of the year. It gets dark so early, it gets light so late, it’s so darn cold. I just think do everything you can to bring yourself joy.”

Though you’d never find them rushing to hang their stockings, the designer duo behind the Kansas City-based firm Kobel + Co, Elizabeth Bennett and Mallory Robins, say there’s one design scheme you can always tap into if you find yourself in the class of outliers running to get decor up before Christmas Eve. “Ralph Lauren Holiday isn't just having a moment; it's a classic holiday go-to for so many reasons,” Bennett explains. “Warm crimsons, rich browns, tactile fabrics, and blue-green plaids make a beautiful holiday palette. Layer in antique silver pieces and simple glass ornaments. A little patina and variation keep everything from feeling too matched. The goal is a warm, welcoming home with pieces that feel collected and personal.” After all, you want it to feel like home, even at Christmas.

#6807. John Legend, Lynn Nottage, and Whitney White on Reclaiming an American Classic From a Black Perspective

The three artists have teamed up on a musical adaptation of Imitation of Life

→ Baca artikel asli

#6806. Justin Theroux Took Me on a Nearly Perfect Trip to Japan. Here’s the Itinerary.

“There’s tourism, and there’s travel,” the actor says. After four days across Tokyo and Kyoto, he reveals exactly what that means.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Justin Theroux carefully holds the brush perpendicular to the sheet of paper. For the past 20-odd minutes, he’s been learning how to press, glide, press a single Japanese kanji character: 永, eternity.

The 55-year-old actor has been practicing each one methodically, including a particularly tricky “airplane” maneuver in which he must hold the brush outward, away from his chest, and guide it gently across the sheet to make a tail that broadens as it travels before ending on a punctuating point at which he must deftly lift his hand away.

Despite praise from his instructor, a renowned Japanese calligrapher called Mamimozi—“Sugoi!” she exclaims, which means amazing—Theroux groans at his subpar airplane attempts. He’s not satisfied with good enough, especially now that the stakes have heightened: He’s been handed a thicker sheet that, once it’s inked with 永, will be framed as a gift to take back home to New York after this trip to Japan.

On this particular journey, he’s partnered with the Luxury Collection, a wide portfolio of award-winning hotels and resorts like the Gritti Palace in Venice and the Royal Hawaiian in Honolulu, to curate experiences like this calligraphy session as a gateway beyond Japan’s typical touristy activities.

“I always feel bad when you see your buses pull up to places and you see people file out, they take pictures, then they get back on the bus, and they go somewhere else,” he tells Esquire. “Anytime you can get access behind walls to a place—that’s what truly unlocks any travel experience.”

The actor has visited Japan numerous times and, over the course of those visits, has connected deeply with Japanese culture. As a “global explorer” and face of the Luxury Collection Expeditions campaign, Theroux has shaped those life-changing travel experiences into two itineraries.

First there’s the Global Explorer Edit: Tokyo with Justin Theroux, based out of the five-star Prince Gallery Tokyo Kioicho hotel, which offers panoramic views of Mount Fuji when the weather’s right. If travelers want to branch out beyond Tokyo to Japan’s imperial capital, the Global Explorer Edit: Kyoto with Justin Theroux offers a different perspective of the Land of the Rising Sun. Guests stay at Hotel the Mitsui, a Michelin three-key accommodation, where modern comforts and historic sensibilities collide for a beyond-memorable stay.

There’s a noticeable theme to Theroux’s itineraries, visible even in the mundane experience of day-to-day Japan. Everyone here, from the citizen on the street to the service worker to the artist, aims to do their very best, much like Theroux does as he makes his first attempt at 永 on the thicker sheet. The streets are litter-free, despite—or because of—the lack of public garbage cans, and the public transit is perpetually, impeccably, on time.

Though Theroux fails to land the airplane this time, there are a few more sheets lying around, a few more chances to try again—and, afterward, a few more stops along his itinerary.

It’s late morning on a partly cloudy September Saturday when Theroux and company—press, PR, friends, guides—leave the Prince Gallery for a speakeasy hidden on the third story of a nondescript building in Tokyo’s Shibuya district. The calligraphy experience awaits inside.

After Theroux decides on his best 永 to frame, Mamimozi, the artist, prepares to perform. She steadies a brush as big as her forearm before making decisive motions, quickly and smoothly, across a sheet the size of a side table. Her movement is entrancing, engrossing—and then, snap, it’s over.

“How does she decide she’s pleased?” Theroux asks Mamimozi’s translator, who passes the question on.

Normally, Mamimozi would do 100 of these before she decides on the best one, the translator relays. This particular character, though? She’s about “92 percent” happy.

“If I ever get to 100 percent,” Mamimozi says through the translator, “that’s the end of my pursuit.” She’s always trying to grow as an artist.

Theroux is presented with his calligraphy in a sturdy frame. Mamimozi having taught Theroux with this particular kanji—永, eternity—suddenly makes a whole lot of sense. “I approach everything artistically, whether it’s writing or acting, as a monastic experience where you’re never going to be completely pleased with everything you do—but you’re always striving for something,” he tells Esquire.

The next pursuit? Scoring fashionable vintage wear, which Tokyo supplies in high volume. Theroux’s Tokyo Edit ensures that travelers are able to take advantage of the spoils of one of the world’s premier fashion markets, focused on four of his favorite shops: Portration, Visvim, Old Joe, and Brackets, all in close proximity in Shibuya. Theroux and Co. then attend the Nanzuka contemporary-art gallery, also in Shibuya, but travelers should expect a visit on the earlier side of their itinerary.

If shopping makes guests hungry, Theroux’s itinerary recommends Yoroniku, an omakase that serves world-class Wagyu. But on this particular trip, Theroux and Co. first head to Bar Centifolia, where “samurai bartender” Yuzo Komai puts on a show that’s earned him the title of world’s best cocktail artist.

When an order for a smoked old-fashioned is received, the house lights go down, leaving a spotlight on Komai. He unleashes a big blue fan and blows air on a large, perfectly clear ice cube to sweat it just a little before unsheathing a katana and chopping it into a trapezoidal prism, which he slides neatly into a rocks glass. Next he pours in a sweet tincture and Beyoncé’s signature SirDavis whiskey and stirs them together with a cocktail spoon. Then he places a circular cut of wood atop and lights the whole damn thing aflame with a blowtorch.

A billow of smoke arises from the concoction, which tastes of sweet and smoky caramelized popcorn. For those less adventurous, the bar owns dozens of rare Japanese whiskies, so delicate and floral and complex that they make the perfect nightcap to relax the shoulders after a dizzying, rewarding day.

To begin the Kyoto itinerary, Theroux finds himself sitting cross-legged in the home studio of third-generation lacquerer Nishimura Keikou. His bowls and cups—which go for $650 a pop—begin their lives as a base of wood whittled to a thickness of less than a millimeter before he begins to layer lacquer, a brownish sap that’s extracted from the tree of the same name. It’s a precious resource, “like the blood of a human,” Keikou says through a translator. The trees do not give much, and once depleted, they die. A single piece of lacquerware can take an entire year to craft, as each of the requisite 14 layers must dry completely before another can be shellacked over top.

Unlike Mamimozi, the calligrapher, Keikou doesn’t consider himself an artist. He doesn’t create because he has an innate need to express; he creates because clients request his goods. Theroux feels a connection nonetheless, especially when Keikou mentions the plight of the woodworkers who make the base of his pieces. They’re behind the camera, so to speak, receiving little glory or credit. Theroux, who is not just an actor but a writer, seems to internalize this as Keikou hands him a bowl as a gift.

Next up on Theroux’s Kyoto itinerary: sushi. His preferred spot, Otachidokoro Sushi Ki, is a stand-up joint where guests can order à la carte or in a set menu. Guests are encouraged to eat with their hands here, which they can rinse between bites using spouts of water that trickle from below a raised edge on the sushi bar.

Theroux’s sushi artisan, befuddlingly, is an Italian immigrant from just outside Milan. How the hell did he get here?

“I came here by plane,” Francesco jokes. Seven years ago, at 27 years old, he committed himself to making sushi at its highest level. Where to go to complete his training? Kyoto, of course, without a return ticket. Now he’s serving Justin Theroux scallop over soy-sauce-soaked rice with a sprinkle of salt and a zest of lime on top.

With bellies full, it’s time for a Zen garden tour. Kyoto is home to many temples and shrines and gardens both Buddhist and Shinto, Japan’s two national religions. But this garden—situated in the Konchi-in Buddhist temple and named, simply, the Crane and Turtle Garden after the shapes of its central rock formations—doesn’t get much foot traffic. It’s more peaceful than the bigger, more popular tourist-mobbed shrines, ideal for a thoughtful traveler like Theroux.

It’s probably “97 out of 100” in terms of popularity, says tour guide Andrew William. Like the sushi chef from earlier, William is not from Japan. He is from Maryland, not too far from Washington, D.C., where Theroux grew up. Like Francesco, he uprooted himself to perfect his art elsewhere, though instead of making sushi, he designs and builds gardens. He’s worked on big projects in the past, including Ben Stiller’s estate on the north shore of Kauai, Hawaii. William’s pursuit, however, belies the reality of chasing dreams: They often turn to nightmares.

William once wanted to be head groundskeeper of this idyllic garden. But it’s not so easy for foreigners to rise in Japanese hierarchies. He claims to have suffered abuse at the hands of his employers and endured long working hours for low pay—“a lot of bad things to maintain beauty,” he says, still apparently chewing on the irony.

Theroux is no stranger to that feeling. Sometimes, he says of acting, “you’ll have a miserable experience that’s received very well. And you think, God, I was so miserable on that job. And yet the product was so good, ’cause so many craftsmen are bringing their talents to bear on it. The editor really helped it, the music really helped it, the director was very good—although they might have been an asshole.”

Theroux and William sit side by side on a broad wooden step that leads into the temple and overlooks the amorphous stony shapes of the crane and the turtle. He and Theroux are perched, William says, on what Buddhist philosophy considers the ship of life. Out directly in front of them is an expanse of pebbles raked into a motif of alternating swirls and straight lines, an ocean of respective rough and smooth seas. On the other side, the crane and turtle await, representing the harmony that anticipates us at the end of our earthly journey.

There is still an evening of activities to go: A visit to the temple. Matcha and handmade sweets in the temple’s tearoom. An intimate geisha dinner complete with drinks, cultural conversation, and traditional Japanese food. All of which prove to be enriching experiences for travelers. Yet it is here, facing the crane and turtle, where visitors can meditate on their journey and appreciate the unique experience on which they’ve embarked.

“There’s tourism, and there’s travel,” Theroux says. “And I think they are two totally different things.”

Tourism is hoofing it from place to place, checking boxes. Seen that, did this. But travel, Theroux’s travel, is different. It’s about meeting people, making friends beside the worker at the front desk or the technician at the spa, and engaging with their lives, their realities, on a deeper level.

“Anytime you can have access to something unique or an area of interest—whether that’s craftsmanship, arts, music, dance, knives, enamelware, things like that—you’re always going to get a little peek into a country or city that you didn’t know existed,” he says.

Like all his endeavors, Theroux’s Japan Expeditions aren’t perfect—if they were, there’d be no reason for visitors to come back—but they’re as close to perfect as it gets.

Both Justin Theroux’s Tokyo and Kyoto Global Explorer Edits are available for booking on the Luxury Collection Expeditions website. The Tokyo Edit is a daylong experience that costs $9,505, while the Kyoto Edit, also a daylong experience, costs $13,145. That price includes a guest, a one-night hotel stay, a private guide, and transportation.

Consider this your sign to swap basic black for cobalt, red, lavender, and more.

#6804. Ariana Grande and Ricky Álvarez Go Red-Carpet Official—at Last!

The pair walked the red carpet arm-in-arm for the world premiere of Focker-in-Law

Things have been moving quickly these past few months for Ariana Grande and Ricky Álvarez. As the story goes, the pair first met over a decade ago, when Álvarez served as a backup dancer on Grande’s Honeymoon Tour. They briefly dated at the time, and made some public appearances together, but broke up by the summer of 2016. Grande even referenced the dalliance in her hit song “thank u, next” (“Wrote some songs about Ricky / Now I listen and laugh”). Flash forward to this past summer, and the pair seemingly had a strong reconnection, being spotted on a number of dates and posing for PDA-filled Instagram posts. Grande even got a new tattoo that winks to the dancer. Now, they’ve just made their red-carpet debut.

Although Grande stepped away from the spotlight for a few months following the end of her Eternal Sunshine Tour on September 1, she made her grand return on Wednesday night to promote her new comedy film, Focker-in-Law, which hits theaters on November 25. And she did so with the support of Álvarez, who walked the red carpet with her at the film’s world premiere at the Beacon Theatre in New York City.

For the occasion, Grande—who was styled by Law Roach—wore a vintage, ’90s-era dress from Badgley Mischka, sourced from Jellybean Vintage.

The gray-and-white plaid design featured a scoop-neck bodice held up by impossibly-thin spaghetti straps, while the A-line skirt flared out in voluminous ruffles.

For a romantic finish, the dress was embroidered with tiny white florals that dotted the neckline, which Grande complemented with a bright-white ribbon holding up her hair bun. She accessorized with silver hoop earrings.

As for Álvarez, he chose a classic white button-up layered underneath an open black blazer and paired with rolled-up, dark-wash jeans, a chunky black belt, and brown loafers.

The lovebirds first sparked reconciliation rumors this past June, when they were spotted together in Austin, Texas, following one of Grande’s Eternal Sunshine shows. Then, Grande essentially confirmed their romance when she began changing the lyrics to “thank u, next” during her concerts.

At one show, she sang: “Wrote some songs about Ricky / I know he’s still got my back,” while at another she crooned: “Wrote some songs about Ricky / We always find our way back.”

But it was the change she made on the final stop of the tour that really seemed to indicate where the couple is at, when Grande sang: “Wrote some songs about Ricky / I’m tryna make him my last.” Now, that’s romantic.

#6803. 13 Clever Details I Saw at the 2026 Kips Bay Decorator Show House in Dallas

Check out the first ever home gym in Kips Bay Show House history.

The seventh annual Kips Bay Decorator Show House in Dallas returns to 2999 Turtle Creek Boulevard, but don’t expect it to look anything like it did in 2025. A new group of the country’s leading designers has taken on the 25,000-square-foot French Renaissance estate, transforming each room to showcase bold ideas, highlight exceptional craftsmanship, and reveal the upcoming trends we can expect to see in the world of interior design.

Visit the show house and see each of the 22 designers’ rooms, from the first dedicated home gym in Kips Bay history by Dallas-based Platinum Gym Design, to a small but mighty European-inspired kitchen by Stephenson Home, to a 1970s-inspired primary bathroom fit with a glass brick shower and curtained-off water closet by Nuance Interiors. With sponsors like Benjamin Moore, The Shade Store, The Rug Company, and more, the vibrant and personality-filled spaces come as no surprise.

As always, proceeds from the show house go to the Kips Bay Boys & Girls Club, which provides educational, enrichment, and developmental programs to thousands of young people, as well as Dallas-based nonprofits Dwell with Dignity and The Crystal Charity Ball.

To see this celebration of exceptional design, you can purchase tickets here and visit the house from Saturday, November 14th to Sunday, December 6th. However, if you can’t make it to the Lone Star State in time, check out some of our favorite moments from the event below.

Kelsi Lamberth of Nuance Interiors pulled inspiration from the 1970s for her primary bathroom, separating the shower from the main prep space using a curved glass brick wall. The sculptural aspects can be found throughout in this groovy space.

In the primary bedroom, Troy Rivington of Rivington Marx Interiors cocooned the room in a dark floral wallpaper and accessorized with other moody botanicals throughout. He was inspired by the romance of 1940s Cairo, dressing the room up like it belonged to a well-traveled collector.

As the first ever dedicated home gym at the Kips Bay Show House, Peter Hoffman of Platinum Gym Design chose a refined, luxurious setup. He named the room Tanren after the Japanese art of hammering and refining metal, and used walnut in both the equipment and the ceiling slats, stainless steel frames, and brown leather accents to complete the exercise space.

In this bespoke bathroom, Russ Peters and Bill Cates of Peters Cates Design lined the wall behind the Studio McGee x Kohler bathtub with a rich merlot tile by RIAD Tile. It’s a beautiful statement and a fabulous way to bring more texture and dimension to the room.

In this tucked away library space, filled with collected finds, designers Christina Garcia Lysaught and Grayson Knight of Layered Dimensions finished off this dark space with a stunning statement mirror. Around the perimeter are resin busts of wildlife, like owls and deer, bringing in a bejeweled touch to the room.

The small but mighty kitchen, designed by Katie Stephenson of Stephenson Home, is crowned with a crackled-gold blooming wallpaper, bringing a warm balance to the rest of the room. It’s covered in birds, butterflies, and blossoms, and it wraps the cook space in a golden glow, reminding all who enter that this room should be as beautiful as it is functional.

The citron paint color wrapping the walls in Jen Bienvenu’s entrance hall is undoubtedly enchanting and serves as the perfect backdrop for her “After the Hunt” theme, but it’s the lampshades made from old silk saris that tie everything together for us. They provide such a magical warmth and really capture the essence of a collector’s treasure room.

Related Story: Almost Every Piece in This Layered Arkansas Home Was a Lucky Antique Find

Step through the citron entry and enter the round room, designed in collaboration by Helen Bergin Interiors and Lichten Architects. The walls are softened by long curtains from The Shade Store, which create beautiful juxtaposition with the curved, sculptural silver media console by Asher Gillman. The actual turntable is flush to the surface, giving it a seamless look while it emits wonderful sound.

In the Withdrawing Room by Jen Stevens of Fonde Interiors, plaster botanicals by artist Amanda Norman come to life as they crawl from the mantel along the chimney breast, escaping to the ceiling. The vines were handmade by Norman and capture the essence of a garden left undisturbed, showing that this formal-at-first-glance room has a wilder side.

The rosy palette in designer Jen Dean’s—founder of Jede Interiors—grand bathroom is topped off with a hand-painted floral mural that covers the ceiling. The rose-gold shine reflects the natural light in the room and give it more depth, while the natural motifs spread across the rest of the space.

Inside this observatory bedroom by sisters Amy Mobley and Katie Norrid of City Hill Design, the Verellen bed is raised up from the ground on a custom platform, emphasizing the importance of the furniture’s role in this space meant for relaxation and rest. It’s wrapped in a striped silk carpet that beckons your touch with a chartreuse mohair sofa at the foot.

Related Story: A Trip to the Lone Star State Inspired the Earthy Textures of This Tennessee New Build

Colorful and collected, this vibrant library by designer Betsy Wentz is not visually quiet. The maximalist space is covered in pattern-mixed Schumacher from ceiling to floor, and the designer pumped up the volume by opting for this triangular table in this tucked away corner.

In designer Amy Switzer’s textural bar lounge—filled with plush chairs, unique artwork, large tassel accessories, and beaded cigarettes—it’s the stone details that really make the room sing. Onyx cornices crown the windows, blue agate inlays decorate the floor, and a banquette which curves around a custom stone and wood table bring a natural coolness to the room that only stone can bring.
